131
Theoretical Framework
130
Theoretical Framework
132
This study is anchored on two theories and models that explain the rationale for skills-based matching and system quality evaluation. The original theoretical foundations were retained because each remains appropriate for the employment platform; their explanations were rewritten in the context of applicants, employers, and PESO administrators.
131
This study is anchored on two theories and models that explain the rationale for skills-based matching and system quality evaluation. The original theoretical foundations were retained because each remains appropriate for the employment platform; their explanations were rewritten in the context of applicants, employers, and PESO administrators.
133
Human Capital and Skills Gap Theory
132
Human Capital and Skills Gap Theory
134
Human Capital Theory (Becker, 1964) holds that education and acquired skills drive an individual’s productivity and economic value. This study uses the theory to justify representing an applicant’s skills as a measurable asset and comparing it with the skills a job requires. By checking the applicant’s record of each required skill, SkillUp identifies mismatches between an applicant’s competencies and a vacancy’s requirements. Because the theory treats training as an investment that raises skills and employability, it also supports recommending training for identified gaps and marking the related skill as PESO-verified after certified training.
133
Human Capital Theory (Becker, 1964) holds that education and acquired skills drive an individual’s productivity and economic value. This study uses the theory to justify representing an applicant’s skills as a measurable asset and comparing it with the skills a job requires. By checking the applicant’s record of each required skill, SkillUp identifies mismatches between an applicant’s competencies and a vacancy’s requirements. Because the theory treats training as an investment that raises skills and employability, it also supports recommending training for identified gaps and marking the related skill as PESO-verified after certified training.
135
Technology Acceptance Model (TAM)
134
Technology Acceptance Model (TAM)
136
The Technology Acceptance Model (Davis, 1989) posits that technology acceptance is driven by Perceived Ease of Use (PEOU) and Perceived Usefulness (PU). In this study, TAM is used to evaluate the acceptance of SkillUp among job applicants, employer representatives, and PESO administrators, focusing on whether they find the platform easy to use, for example creating a profile, rating skills, posting a vacancy, or reviewing candidates, and useful, for example in job matching, skill gap information, candidate evaluation, and training recommendation.
135
The Technology Acceptance Model (Davis, 1989) posits that technology acceptance is driven by Perceived Ease of Use (PEOU) and Perceived Usefulness (PU). In this study, TAM is used to evaluate the acceptance of SkillUp among job applicants, employer representatives, and PESO administrators, focusing on whether they find the platform easy to use, for example creating a profile, rating skills, posting a vacancy, or reviewing candidates, and useful, for example in job matching, skill gap information, candidate evaluation, and training recommendation.
137
ISO/IEC 25010:2023 Software Quality Model
136
ISO/IEC 25010:2023 Software Quality Model
138
The ISO/IEC 25010:2023 model evaluates system quality through functional suitability, performance efficiency, compatibility, interaction capability, reliability, security, maintainability, and flexibility (and their sub-characteristics). It is used to assess the technical quality of SkillUp as a web application serving multiple user roles, and it is applied primarily by IT expert evaluators.
137
The ISO/IEC 25010:2023 model evaluates system quality through functional suitability, performance efficiency, compatibility, interaction capability, reliability, security, maintainability, and flexibility (and their sub-characteristics). It is used to assess the technical quality of SkillUp as a web application serving multiple user roles, and it is applied primarily by IT expert evaluators.
139
Together, these frameworks guide the development, validation, and evaluation of the platform by ensuring that it is relevant to the skills-employment problem (Human Capital and Skills Gap Theory), acceptable to its users (TAM), and consistent with recognized software quality standards (ISO/IEC 25010:2023). No additional theory was introduced.
138
Together, these frameworks guide the development, validation, and evaluation of the platform by ensuring that it is relevant to the skills-employment problem (Human Capital and Skills Gap Theory), acceptable to its users (TAM), and consistent with recognized software quality standards (ISO/IEC 25010:2023). No additional theory was introduced.
140
Integration of the Framework
139
Integration of the Framework
141
The three frameworks describe one chain rather than three separate justifications. Human Capital Theory explains why the skills record matters: if skills are the productive asset that a labour market rewards, then a platform that records them, makes them comparable, and points the holder towards the training that closes a specific gap has economic value for the applicant and for the employer. The Technology Acceptance Model explains what determines whether the people who must use that record will adopt it: an applicant, an employer representative, or a PESO administrator will rely on the platform only if they find it easy to use (Perceived Ease of Use) and useful for matching, gap analysis, candidate review, and training recommendation (Perceived Usefulness). ISO/IEC 25010:2023 explains how the artefact that does this is to be judged: quality is not asserted but measured against nine characteristics with defined sub-characteristics, so a claim that the platform is usable or reliable has to be supported by ratings rather than by description.
140
The three frameworks describe one chain rather than three separate justifications. Human Capital Theory explains why the skills record matters: if skills are the productive asset that a labour market rewards, then a platform that records them, makes them comparable, and points the holder towards the training that closes a specific gap has economic value for the applicant and for the employer. The Technology Acceptance Model explains what determines whether the people who must use that record will adopt it: an applicant, an employer representative, or a PESO administrator will rely on the platform only if they find it easy to use (Perceived Ease of Use) and useful for matching, gap analysis, candidate review, and training recommendation (Perceived Usefulness). ISO/IEC 25010:2023 explains how the artefact that does this is to be judged: quality is not asserted but measured against eight characteristics with defined sub-characteristics, so a claim that the platform is usable or reliable has to be supported by ratings rather than by description.
142
Conceptual Framework
141
Conceptual Framework
143
This study adopts the Input-Process-Output (IPO) model with Feedback, guided by the Iterative Waterfall Model as the System Development Life Cycle (SDLC) framework. The Iterative Waterfall Model supports systematic, structured development while allowing refinements at each phase.
142
This study adopts the Input-Process-Output (IPO) model with Feedback, guided by the Iterative Waterfall Model as the System Development Life Cycle (SDLC) framework. The Iterative Waterfall Model supports systematic, structured development while allowing refinements at each phase.

Process
145
Process
147
The process follows the Iterative Waterfall SDLC through requirements analysis, system design, development, testing, and implementation. Within the platform, the process includes account registration and verification; applicant profile and skill management; employer profile and vacancy management with required-skill mapping; skill-based matching; skill gap analysis; job discovery and application processing; employer candidate review, interview scheduling, and offer and hiring; PESO referral, pre-employment compliance, and retention monitoring; training recommendation, enrollment, and certification; notifications; analytics and reporting; and system evaluation using ISO/IEC 25010:2023.

Output

Output

The output is the developed SkillUp platform and the information it produces: matched job opportunities and compatibility scores; skill gap reports; training recommendations; application, interview, and hiring status; candidate information for employers; dashboards and exportable reports; and the evaluation results from ISO/IEC 25010:2023 [pending data collection].

Feedback
149
Feedback
151
Feedback consists of testing results and IT expert evaluation results gathered after each system iteration through testing and the ISO/IEC 25010:2023 evaluation. This feedback is analyzed to identify strengths and weaknesses and is used to refine subsequent iterations. A second feedback loop exists within the platform: after PESO certifies training completion, the newly PESO-verified skill is reflected in the applicant’s subsequent matching results.

152
Variables and Relationships
151
Variables and Relationships
153
The independent variable is the SkillUp platform as implemented: its sixteen modules, its deterministic matching function, its training loop, and its three role-specific interfaces. The dependent variables are the software quality of the platform, measured through the nine ISO/IEC 25010:2023 characteristics, and the operational outputs the platform produces, namely match scores, gap reports, training recommendations, referral status, and retention records. Applicant, employer, and vacancy characteristics act as conditions rather than controlled variables, because the mix of skills in the registry, the quality of an employer's vacancy specification, and the size of the applicant pool all change what the platform produces, and the study does not manipulate them.
152

161
Conceptual Definitions
161
Conceptual Definitions
162
Applicant. A person seeking employment or upskilling who registers on the platform. In Philippine public employment practice the applicant is the jobseeker whose profile is referred to an employer (Department of Labor and Employment, 2016).
162
Applicant. A person seeking employment or upskilling who registers on the platform. In Philippine public employment practice the applicant is the jobseeker whose profile is referred to an employer (Department of Labor and Employment, 2016).
163
Competency. A skill, knowledge, or attitude that enables a person to perform a task to a defined standard, and which technical-vocational training is designed to develop and certify (Technical Education and Skills Development Authority, 2023).
163
Competency. A skill, knowledge, or attitude that enables a person to perform a task to a defined standard, and which technical-vocational training is designed to develop and certify (Technical Education and Skills Development Authority, 2023).
164
Composite match percentage. A single figure summarising how well an applicant's record corresponds to a vacancy's stated requirements. It is a form of person-job fit measure, and person-job recommendation research treats the decomposability of such a measure as the condition of its usefulness to users (Tang et al., 2025).
164
Composite match percentage. A single figure summarising how well an applicant's record corresponds to a vacancy's stated requirements. It is a form of person-job fit measure, and person-job recommendation research treats the decomposability of such a measure as the condition of its usefulness to users (Tang et al., 2025).
165
Employer. An enterprise or hiring manager that posts vacancies and selects among applicants. In the public employment system the employer is a registered partner whose vacancies are published and whose placements are reported through the employment office (Department of Labor and Employment, 2016).
165
Employer. An enterprise or hiring manager that posts vacancies and selects among applicants. In the public employment system the employer is a registered partner whose vacancies are published and whose placements are reported through the employment office (Department of Labor and Employment, 2016).
166
Gap analysis. The identification of the specific requirements of a role that a candidate does not yet meet. Skills-gap analysis is the diagnostic step that precedes targeted training, and its value depends on the requirements and the candidate's skills being expressed in a comparable vocabulary (Braun et al., 2024b).
166
Gap analysis. The identification of the specific requirements of a role that a candidate does not yet meet. Skills-gap analysis is the diagnostic step that precedes targeted training, and its value depends on the requirements and the candidate's skills being expressed in a comparable vocabulary (Braun et al., 2024b).
167
Public Employment Service Office (PESO). A local government unit office that facilitates employment through registration, referral, training coordination, and placement monitoring, operating within the framework set by the Department of Labor and Employment (Department of Labor and Employment, 2016).
167
Public Employment Service Office (PESO). A local government unit office that facilitates employment through registration, referral, training coordination, and placement monitoring, operating within the framework set by the Department of Labor and Employment (Department of Labor and Employment, 2016).
168
Referral. The formal endorsement of an applicant to a vacancy by an employment office, which creates a record that the office is expected to monitor through placement (Department of Labor and Employment, 2016).
168
Referral. The formal endorsement of an applicant to a vacancy by an employment office, which creates a record that the office is expected to monitor through placement (Department of Labor and Employment, 2016).
169
Skill provenance. The record of how a claim to a skill was obtained, whether through self-report, assessment, employer or office verification, document extraction, or completed training. Because self-assessments track ability unevenly, provenance is a condition for interpreting any skills claim (Adamecz et al., 2025).
169
Skill provenance. The record of how a claim to a skill was obtained, whether through self-report, assessment, employer or office verification, document extraction, or completed training. Because self-assessments track ability unevenly, provenance is a condition for interpreting any skills claim (Adamecz et al., 2025).
170
Transferable skill. A skill that satisfies a requirement without matching it exactly, by belonging to the same family of technologies or practices. The concept underlies competency-based matching in which partial credit is given for related evidence (Hien et al., 2024).
170
Transferable skill. A skill that satisfies a requirement without matching it exactly, by belonging to the same family of technologies or practices. The concept underlies competency-based matching in which partial credit is given for related evidence (Hien et al., 2024).

Operational Definitions
171
Operational Definitions
172
Applicant. A user whose profile carries the role applicant, who completes the profile wizard, records skills with provenance, applies to vacancies, and enrols in training.
172
Applicant. A user whose profile carries the role applicant, who completes the profile wizard, records skills with provenance, applies to vacancies, and enrols in training.
173
Competency. A row in the Centralized Skills Registry, one of 789 skills across more than 20 categories, linked to an applicant through an applicant-skill record carrying a provenance value.
173
Competency. A row in the Centralized Skills Registry, one of 789 skills across more than 20 categories, linked to an applicant through an applicant-skill record carrying a provenance value.
174
Composite match percentage. The score returned by the matching function: 0.60 of the skill component, 0.20 of the education component, and 0.20 of the experience component, clamped to 0 to 100 and then adjusted for career alignment. Each required skill is scored 1.0 for an exact match, 0.5 for a transferable match, and 0.0 for no match.
174
Composite match percentage. The score returned by the matching function: 0.60 of the skill component, 0.20 of the education component, and 0.20 of the experience component, clamped to 0 to 100 and then adjusted for career alignment. Each required skill is scored 1.0 for an exact match, 0.5 for a transferable match, and 0.0 for no match.
175
Employer. A user whose profile carries the role employer, who maintains a company profile, posts vacancies with required skills drawn from the registry, and moves candidates through the hiring pipeline.
175
Employer. A user whose profile carries the role employer, who maintains a company profile, posts vacancies with required skills drawn from the registry, and moves candidates through the hiring pipeline.
176
Gap analysis. The per-skill output of the matching function, which classifies every required skill of a chosen vacancy as an exact, transferable, or missing match and displays the result as a radar chart and a gap list.
176
Gap analysis. The per-skill output of the matching function, which classifies every required skill of a chosen vacancy as an exact, transferable, or missing match and displays the result as a radar chart and a gap list.
177
Public Employment Service Office (PESO). A user whose profile carries the role admin, who verifies employers, creates referrals, authors training curricula, audits pre-employment compliance, records probation and retention outcomes, and exports reports.
177
Public Employment Service Office (PESO). A user whose profile carries the role admin, who verifies employers, creates referrals, authors training curricula, audits pre-employment compliance, records probation and retention outcomes, and exports reports.
178
Referral. A single record linking one applicant to one vacancy, created either by the applicant's own application or by a PESO referral, and carrying the status, interview stages, offer, pre-employment documents, probation outcome, and post-placement evaluations.
178
Referral. A single record linking one applicant to one vacancy, created either by the applicant's own application or by a PESO referral, and carrying the status, interview stages, offer, pre-employment documents, probation outcome, and post-placement evaluations.
179
Skill provenance. The source field on an applicant-skill record, which takes one of six values: self-reported, assessment-verified, PESO-verified, résumé-extracted, training-completed, or system-seeded.
179
Skill provenance. The source field on an applicant-skill record, which takes one of six values: self-reported, assessment-verified, PESO-verified, résumé-extracted, training-completed, or system-seeded.
180
Transferable skill. A required skill satisfied through a curated group in the matching function, such as SQL, front-end framework, testing, cloud, containers, version control, agile, authentication, CSS, or programming language, and scored at the transferable weight.

CHAPTER 2 — SYSTEM DEVELOPMENT AND METHODOLOGY
182
SYSTEM DEVELOPMENT AND METHODOLOGY
183
This chapter presents how the SkillUp platform was developed and the research methodology used to evaluate it. It first describes the research design, the respondents and setting, the research instruments, the data gathering procedure, the statistical treatment, and the ethical considerations. It then covers the development approach, requirements analysis, system design, implementation, tools, schedule, responsibilities, and testing, and it serves as the technical foundation of the capstone project.
182
This chapter presents how the SkillUp platform was developed and the research methodology used to evaluate it. It first describes the research design, the respondents and setting, the research instruments, the data gathering procedure, the statistical treatment, and the ethical considerations. It then covers the development approach, requirements analysis, system design, implementation, tools, schedule, responsibilities, and testing, and it serves as the technical foundation of the capstone project.

Research Design
183
Research Design
185
A quantitative, descriptive approach is used to develop and evaluate SkillUp. Numerical data are collected to quantify the system’s software quality. Descriptive research is appropriate because it goes beyond data collection to include comparison, measurement, and evaluation, allowing the researchers to objectively assess the system from structured survey responses.
184
A quantitative, descriptive approach is used to develop and evaluate SkillUp. Numerical data are collected to quantify the system’s software quality. Descriptive research is appropriate because it goes beyond data collection to include comparison, measurement, and evaluation, allowing the researchers to objectively assess the system from structured survey responses.
186
Developmental Component
185
Developmental Component
187
The developmental component produced the SkillUp platform. It followed the five phases described under System Development Methodology, and each phase produced the artefact on which the next depended. The component is developmental rather than experimental because the researchers built the artefact under study; the aim was not to compare two treatments but to produce a working system and to determine whether it met its specified functions and quality criteria.
186
The developmental component produced the SkillUp platform. It followed the five phases described under System Development Methodology, and each phase produced the artefact on which the next depended. The component is developmental rather than experimental because the researchers built the artefact under study; the aim was not to compare two treatments but to produce a working system and to determine whether it met its specified functions and quality criteria.
188
Descriptive Component
187
Descriptive Component
189
The descriptive component documented what the system does and how the evaluators responded to it. It covers the sixteen implemented modules, the three role-specific portals, the matching and gap functions, the training loop, and the compliance and retention workflow, together with the profile of the respondents and the conditions under which the evaluation was conducted. Recording those conditions matters because the evaluation took place on a demonstration deployment with five IT experts rather than under a controlled comparison.
188
The descriptive component documented what the system does and how the evaluators responded to it. It covers the sixteen implemented modules, the three role-specific portals, the matching and gap functions, the training loop, and the compliance and retention workflow, together with the profile of the respondents and the conditions under which the evaluation was conducted. Recording those conditions matters because the evaluation took place on a demonstration deployment with five IT experts rather than under a controlled comparison.
190
Quantitative Component
189
Quantitative Component
191
The quantitative component used the validated evaluation questionnaire to rate the platform against the nine ISO/IEC 25010:2023 characteristics on a four-point scale. Responses were summarised as weighted means per indicator, per characteristic, and overall, and interpreted against fixed intervals. The design is cross-sectional: each evaluator completed the instrument once, after examining all three role interfaces. It is neither longitudinal nor controlled, so the results describe the platform at the point of evaluation.
190
The quantitative component used the validated evaluation questionnaire to rate the platform against the nine ISO/IEC 25010:2023 characteristics on a four-point scale. Responses were summarised as weighted means per indicator, per characteristic, and overall, and interpreted against fixed intervals. The design is cross-sectional: each evaluator completed the instrument once, after examining all three role interfaces. It is neither longitudinal nor controlled, so the results describe the platform at the point of evaluation.
192
Why the Design Fits
191
Why the Design Fits
193
The design meets the three demands of the study. The developmental demand is met by building and testing the platform, the descriptive demand by documenting the implemented modules and the evaluation setting, and the quantitative demand by measuring quality with a structured instrument. The design also permits triangulation: the functional test results, the expert quality ratings, and the recorded system behaviour are different forms of evidence, and agreement among them is more informative than any single source. A high quality rating cannot compensate for a failed test case, and a passing test cannot establish that evaluators found the system usable.Research Subjects
192
The design meets the three demands of the study. The developmental demand is met by building and testing the platform, the descriptive demand by documenting the implemented modules and the evaluation setting, and the quantitative demand by measuring quality with a structured instrument. The design also permits triangulation: the functional test results, the expert quality ratings, and the recorded system behaviour are different forms of evidence, and agreement among them is more informative than any single source. A high quality rating cannot compensate for a failed test case, and a passing test cannot establish that evaluators found the system usable.
194
This study uses a quantitative research design; therefore, the participants are referred to as respondents. The respondents are IT experts, who evaluate the technical quality of SkillUp. A total of 5 IT experts will participate (Table 1). Job applicants, employer representatives, and PESO administrators are the intended users of the platform, but they are not respondents of this study. Each IT expert examines the features of all three user roles and evaluates the full set of ISO/IEC 25010:2023 characteristics: functional suitability, performance efficiency, compatibility, interaction capability, reliability, security, maintainability, and flexibility.

The second group is composed of five IT experts who examined all three role interfaces and evaluated the platform against the eight in-scope ISO/IEC 25010:2023 characteristics. The two groups are separate: the applicants provide user-acceptance data, while the IT experts provide software-quality data.

The study uses nonprobability sampling, specifically purposive sampling. This is a technique in which participants are chosen intentionally based on their knowledge, experience, or involvement with the subject being studied (Creswell, 2014). It is appropriate here because evaluating software quality against ISO/IEC 25010:2023 requires respondents with technical knowledge of software development or software quality. Because the sample is not random, the number of respondents was set by the researchers rather than computed with a sampling formula. The inclusion criteria are as follows:

Research Instruments
209
Research Instruments
206
The study uses one data-gathering instrument: a structured evaluation questionnaire. It is originally designed rather than adopted or adapted from an existing standardised tool: the researchers built it from the objectives of the study and aligned it with the nine characteristics of the ISO/IEC 25010:2023 software quality model. It is divided into three parts. Part I records the respondent's professional profile, namely current position, area of expertise, and years of experience in software development or software quality. Part II holds closed-ended statements on functional suitability and interaction capability, asking whether the system performs the functions each user role requires and whether it can be used effectively. Part III holds closed-ended statements on reliability and performance efficiency, asking whether the system returns consistent results and responds within an acceptable time. The IT experts additionally rate the remaining characteristics, namely compatibility, security, maintainability, and flexibility, so that all nine are covered. Every item is a closed-ended statement rated on a four-point Likert scale (4 = Excellent, 3 = Good, 2 = Fair, 1 = Poor); no open-ended or interview item is used, and the items ask respondents to judge a named feature of the system rather than to describe their own practice.
210
The study used two instruments. The Technology Acceptance Model (TAM) questionnaire measured applicant acceptance of SkillUp. The ISO/IEC 25010:2023 evaluation questionnaire measured the software quality of the platform as judged by IT experts. Both instruments used the same four-point agreement scale and were administered separately.
207
Table 2.2 Four-Point Likert Scale for TAM
211
Employer and PESO evaluation was not conducted. The employer and PESO groups took part in requirements consultation only. One PESO response was received, but a single response cannot support an evaluation and is excluded from the results. TAM therefore measures only the applicant entity; the ISO evaluation measures the expert view of the platform as a whole.

TABLE | Numerical value | Verbal Interpretation | General Meaning
212
Technology Acceptance Model Questionnaire
213
The TAM questionnaire was adapted from Davis (1989) and contextualized to SkillUp’s skills-based matching, gap analysis, training recommendation, and hiring functions. It contained 12 items: six Perceived Usefulness (PU) items and six Perceived Ease of Use (PEOU) items. The PU items covered skill-based matching, gap analysis, match scores, training recommendations, hiring management, and overall usefulness. The PEOU items covered learning, registration and OTP verification, skill selection, reading match and gap results, task completion, and overall ease of use. The instrument was administered to the 15 applicant respondents after they used the applicant portal.
214
ISO/IEC 25010:2023 Evaluation Questionnaire
215
The ISO/IEC 25010:2023 questionnaire contained 49 statements covering the eight in-scope product-quality characteristics: functional suitability, performance efficiency, compatibility, interaction capability, reliability, security, maintainability, and flexibility. Safety was not part of the instrument or the evaluation. The five IT experts examined the applicant, employer, and PESO interfaces and rated each statement after inspection and demonstration.

Data Gathering Procedure
223
Data Gathering Procedure
222
First, the researchers secure permission from the adviser and from the participating organizations to conduct the study and identify respondents using purposive sampling. Respondents are informed of the purpose of the study and their role in the evaluation, and their consent is obtained.
224
First, the researchers secure permission from the adviser and from the participating organizations to conduct the study and identify respondents using purposive sampling. Respondents are informed of the purpose of the study and their role in the evaluation, and their consent is obtained.
223
Next, the IT experts examine the developed system across all three user roles. As job applicants, they create a profile, add their skills, view job matches and skill gaps, apply for a vacancy, and view training recommendations. As employer representatives, they create a company profile and vacancy with required skills, review candidates and their gap analysis, and use the interview and hiring functions. As PESO administrators, they use verification, referral, training, tracking, and reporting functions. After examining the system, they answer the questionnaire to evaluate its software quality characteristics, either in printed form or through online forms depending on accessibility.
225
Next, the IT experts examine the developed system across all three user roles. As job applicants, they create a profile, add their skills, view job matches and skill gaps, apply for a vacancy, and view training recommendations. As employer representatives, they create a company profile and vacancy with required skills, review candidates and their gap analysis, and use the interview and hiring functions. As PESO administrators, they use verification, referral, training, tracking, and reporting functions. After examining the system, they answer the questionnaire to evaluate its software quality characteristics, either in printed form or through online forms depending on accessibility.
224
The researchers then collect and review the questionnaires for completeness and validity and encode and then organize the responses using the Statistical Package for the Social Sciences (SPSS). Descriptive statistics are used to analyze the data according to the objectives of the study. All data are used strictly for academic purposes and handled confidentially.

The study uses a four-point Likert scale. For the ISO/IEC 25010:2023 evaluation, respondents choose Excellent (4), Good (3), Fair (2), or Poor (1). The weighted mean is computed per item, and the following scale is used to interpret it.

The study uses a four-point agreement scale. For the ISO/IEC 25010:2023 evaluation, respondents choose Strongly Agree (4), Agree (3), Disagree (2), or Strongly Disagree (1). The weighted mean is computed per item, and the following scale is used to interpret it.

A characteristic whose mean is significantly greater than 2.50 at the 0.05 level of significance is interpreted as meeting the acceptability threshold. Where the normality assumption is not met, the Wilcoxon signed-rank test is substituted for the one-sample t-test.

The mean is computed with the formula μ = (Σfx) / n, where μ is the mean of each evaluation; Σfx is the sum of the products of f, the frequency of each weight, and x, the weight (4, 3, 2, or 1); and n is the total number of respondents.

Expert Ratings
237
Expert Ratings
236
The five IT experts provided one rating per statement together with an overall judgement. The analysis computed the weighted mean and the standard deviation for each indicator, for each characteristic, and for the grand mean across characteristics. No inferential test was applied to the expert ratings, because the panel was purposive and small; the ratings are interpreted descriptively and read alongside the functional test results rather than tested for significance against each other.
238
The five IT experts provided one rating per statement together with an overall judgement. The analysis computed the weighted mean and the standard deviation for each indicator, for each characteristic, and for the grand mean across characteristics. A one-sample t-test was then used only as a benchmark against the 2.50 midpoint. Each evaluator’s mean for a characteristic formed one observation, giving five observations and four degrees of freedom per characteristic. Because the panel was purposive and small, the test is interpreted as a descriptive benchmark alongside the functional test results, not as a population-level estimate.
237
Functional Test Data
239
Functional Test Data
238
Functional testing produced pass or fail outcomes rather than survey scores. The outcomes were summarised by module and by test case, and they were used to contextualise the expert ratings: a module that failed a case was noted even where the corresponding quality rating was high, and a characteristic that drew a high rating was read against the cases that exercised it.

Interpretation and Limits
241
Interpretation and Limits
240
The analysis has no qualitative thematic component. Comments offered during the evaluation were used only as context for the quantitative results, not as a separately coded dataset. The study also does not model the relationship between the quality ratings and placement outcomes, because the evaluation window was too short to observe placements that any rated improvement could have influenced, and the rating therefore describes the software rather than its labour-market effect.

System Development Methodology
251
System Development Methodology
250
This study uses the Iterative Waterfall Model as the System Development Life Cycle (SDLC) in developing SkillUp. The Iterative Waterfall Model is a structured approach that follows a sequential progression of phases while allowing developers to revisit earlier stages for refinement. It addresses the limitations of the traditional waterfall model by incorporating feedback loops between phases.
252
This study uses the Iterative Waterfall Model as the System Development Life Cycle (SDLC) in developing SkillUp. The Iterative Waterfall Model is a structured approach that follows a sequential progression of phases while allowing developers to revisit earlier stages for refinement. It addresses the limitations of the traditional waterfall model by incorporating feedback loops between phases.
251
Structured systems analysis and design follow a disciplined process in which each phase is completed before proceeding, ensuring clarity, control, and reduced development errors. Software engineering practice also recognizes that real-world development often requires revisiting earlier stages when issues are found during testing or implementation, and that combining structured phases with iterative feedback improves reliability, reduces defects, and enhances software quality.
253
Structured systems analysis and design follow a disciplined process in which each phase is completed before proceeding, ensuring clarity, control, and reduced development errors. Software engineering practice also recognizes that real-world development often requires revisiting earlier stages when issues are found during testing or implementation, and that combining structured phases with iterative feedback improves reliability, reduces defects, and enhances software quality.
252
Figure 2.1 Iterative Waterfall Software Development Life Cycle
254
Figure 2.1. Iterative Waterfall Software Development Life Cycle
253
The model was chosen because SkillUp involves several interdependent, rule-based components (skill data, vacancy requirements, matching, gap analysis, application and hiring workflow, and training recommendation) that must each be validated before the next depends on them, while still allowing controlled iteration to improve the accuracy of match and gap results, the relevance of training recommendations, and the usability of each role’s portal.
255
The model was chosen because SkillUp involves several interdependent, rule-based components (skill data, vacancy requirements, matching, gap analysis, application and hiring workflow, and training recommendation) that must each be validated before the next depends on them, while still allowing controlled iteration to improve the accuracy of match and gap results, the relevance of training recommendations, and the usability of each role’s portal.
254
Development followed five iterative phases. In the Requirements Analysis Phase, the researchers gathered and analyzed the needs of job applicants, employers, and PESO administrators, and identified functional and non-functional requirements. In the System Design Phase, the architecture, database structure, process flows, and user interface designs for the three role portals were developed. In the System Development Phase, the system was implemented in Python with the Django web framework, including the account, profile and skill, vacancy, matching and gap analysis, application and hiring, training, PESO coordination, notification, and analytics modules. In the Testing Phase, unit, integration, system, and user acceptance testing were planned to identify errors and verify functionality, reliability, and performance, with issues fed back into earlier phases. In the Implementation Phase, the system is deployed in a controlled environment where users are oriented to the platform, and their feedback informs further refinement.
256

In the Requirements Analysis Phase, the researchers gathered and analyzed the needs of job applicants, employers, and PESO administrators, and identified functional and non-functional requirements. In the System Development Phase, the system was implemented in Python with the Django web framework, including the account, profile and skill, vacancy, matching and gap analysis, application and hiring, training, PESO coordination, notification, and analytics modules. In the Testing Phase, unit, integration, system, and user acceptance testing were planned to identify errors and verify functionality, reliability, and performance, with issues fed back into earlier phases. In the Implementation Phase, the system is deployed in a controlled environment where users are oriented to the platform, and their feedback informs further refinement.

Functional Requirements
259
Functional Requirements
258
Functional requirements state what the system must do for each of its three user roles. They were derived from the needs of job applicants, employers, and PESO administrators gathered during the requirements analysis phase, and each is stated together with the system function that satisfies it. Tables 7 to 9 list them by role. The applicant requirements cover account onboarding, profile and skill evidence, job search and fit reasoning, gap inspection, application, training and certification, compliance document upload, and the personal dashboard. The employer requirements cover the company profile, vacancy management and benchmark skill mapping, ranked candidate review, gap analysis, interview scheduling, offer extension, slot management, and the recruitment dashboard. The PESO requirements cover employer accreditation, talent oversight, vacancy and referral monitoring, AI-assisted curriculum generation, training and certification management, pre-employment compliance auditing, retention audits, and labour analytics with statutory export.

Non-Functional Requirements
302
Non-Functional Requirements
301
The non-functional requirements are: (1) Usability—each role has a dedicated, responsive portal with role-appropriate functions, semantic HTML and labelled form fields; a WCAG 2.1 audit is planned and has not yet been performed; (2) Reliability and Determinism—compatibility calculations are reproducible, and procedural fallbacks ensure uninterrupted operation when external AI APIs are unavailable; (3) Security—role-based view guards prevent privilege escalation, OTP verification guards account creation, PBKDF2 password hashing secures credentials, and uploaded files are restricted by type and size, with identification documents encrypted at rest; (4) Performance Efficiency—matching is computed at request time and related records are prefetched through the Django ORM; no load test has been run, and the employer dashboard's applicant-by-vacancy scoring is recorded as the scaling limit; and (5) Maintainability—the codebase is modular, with business logic, templates, static assets, and curriculum definitions cleanly separated.
303
The non-functional requirements are: (1) Usability—each role has a dedicated, responsive portal with role-appropriate functions, semantic HTML and labelled form fields; a WCAG 2.1 audit is planned and has not yet been performed; (2) Reliability and Determinism—compatibility calculations are reproducible, and procedural fallbacks ensure uninterrupted operation when external AI APIs are unavailable; (3) Security—role-based view guards prevent privilege escalation, OTP verification guards account creation, PBKDF2 password hashing secures credentials, and uploaded files are restricted by type and size, with identification documents encrypted at rest; (4) Performance Efficiency—matching is computed at request time and related records are prefetched through the Django ORM; no load test has been run, and the employer dashboard's applicant-by-vacancy scoring is recorded as the scaling limit; and (5) Maintainability—the codebase is modular, with business logic, templates, static assets, and curriculum definitions cleanly separated.

Requirements Analysis
304
Requirements Analysis
303
Existing System
305
Existing System
304
In conventional practice, job seeking and recruitment are only partly supported by structured skills information. Job seekers typically look for vacancies through postings, walk-in or referral channels, and general job listings, and apply with a résumé that describes their skills in free text. Employers and employment offices screen these résumés and applications largely by hand, comparing them with the job description by reading and judgment. Because skills are not recorded on a common skills taxonomy, it is difficult for an applicant to know how well they fit a vacancy or what they would need to learn, and difficult for an employer to compare candidates consistently. Employment offices coordinating referrals, training, and follow-up must track these activities across separate records.
306
In conventional practice, job seeking and recruitment are only partly supported by structured skills information. Job seekers typically look for vacancies through postings, walk-in or referral channels, and general job listings, and apply with a résumé that describes their skills in free text. Employers and employment offices screen these résumés and applications largely by hand, comparing them with the job description by reading and judgment. Because skills are not recorded on a common skills taxonomy, it is difficult for an applicant to know how well they fit a vacancy or what they would need to learn, and difficult for an employer to compare candidates consistently. Employment offices coordinating referrals, training, and follow-up must track these activities across separate records.

Proposed System
310
Proposed System
309
The proposed system is SkillUp, a web-based employment and talent management platform that connects job applicants and employers through structured skills data and provides PESO administrators with coordination and municipal labor intelligence tools. It centralizes applicant profiles and recorded competencies, employer vacancies and benchmark skill requirements, deterministic compatibility matching, skill gap radar diagnostics, a three-stage recruitment workflow, a native interactive Learning Management System (LMS) with publicly verifiable certification, and post-placement probation and retention monitoring on one integrated platform.
311
The proposed system is SkillUp, a web-based employment and talent management platform that connects job applicants and employers through structured skills data and provides PESO administrators with coordination and municipal labor intelligence tools. It centralizes applicant profiles and recorded competencies, employer vacancies and benchmark skill requirements, deterministic compatibility matching, skill gap radar diagnostics, a three-stage recruitment workflow, a native interactive Learning Management System (LMS) with publicly verifiable certification, and post-placement probation and retention monitoring on one integrated platform.
310
Missing skills identified by the gap engine are cross-referenced with available training programs, generating ranked recommendations for applicants. Applicants self-enroll in courses managed through the native interactive Learning Management System (LMS). The LMS course player delivers video streaming lessons, technical standard operating procedure (SOP) reading guides, interactive module checkpoint quizzes, and heartbeat session timers that track time spent. Applicants complete an end-of-course capstone examination with a 70% passing threshold. Upon passing, the system records the completed course as training-verified evidence on the relevant skills, creates a certification flagged as TESDA, and issues a certificate bearing a verification code in the form SKILLUP-TESDA-2026-XXXXXX that anyone can check on the public verification page.
312
Missing skills identified by the gap engine are cross-referenced with available training programs, generating ranked recommendations for applicants. Applicants self-enroll in courses managed through the native interactive Learning Management System (LMS). The LMS course player delivers video streaming lessons, technical standard operating procedure (SOP) reading guides, interactive module checkpoint quizzes, and heartbeat session timers that track time spent. Applicants complete an end-of-course capstone examination with a 70% passing threshold. Upon passing, the system records the completed course as training-verified evidence on the relevant skills, creates a certification flagged as TESDA, and issues a certificate bearing a verification code in the form SKILLUP-TESDA-2026-XXXXXX that anyone can check on the public verification page.
311
The platform employs a hybrid architecture: Google Gemini Generative AI assists with eight bounded tasks, namely résumé extraction, identification-document extraction, job recommendation, qualification-fit explanation, training recommendation, curriculum generation, syllabus import, and video alignment, each with a deterministic fallback, while all compatibility matching and deficiency calculations remain strictly rule-based and deterministic. Employers retain full hiring discretion across a structured three-stage interview pipeline. Role-specific portals present applicants, employers, and PESO administrators with their dedicated tools, while real-time in-app notifications keep all stakeholders informed of matches, interviews, offers, and compliance milestones.
313
The platform employs a hybrid architecture: Google Gemini Generative AI assists with eight bounded tasks, namely résumé extraction, identification-document extraction, job recommendation, qualification-fit explanation, training recommendation, curriculum generation, syllabus import, and video alignment, each with a deterministic fallback, while all compatibility matching and deficiency calculations remain strictly rule-based and deterministic. Employers retain full hiring discretion across a structured three-stage interview pipeline. Role-specific portals present applicants, employers, and PESO administrators with their dedicated tools, while real-time in-app notifications keep all stakeholders informed of matches, interviews, offers, and compliance milestones.

Data Flow Diagram
318
Data Flow Diagram
317
The data flow diagram shows the inputs and outputs of each external entity and process. A data flow diagram uses four basic elements: processes, data stores, external entities, and data flows. A context diagram, also called a level 0 data flow diagram, presents the whole system as a single process with its external entities.
319
The data flow diagram shows the inputs and outputs of each external entity and process. A data flow diagram uses four basic elements: processes, data stores, external entities, and data flows. A context diagram, also called a level 0 data flow diagram, presents the whole system as a single process with its external entities.
318
The level 0 diagram (Figure 2.8) shows three external entities interacting with SkillUp. The Job Applicant submits registration, profile, and skill data, applications, training enrollments, and pre-employment documents, and receives match scores, skill gap reports, training recommendations, application status, and notifications. The Employer submits company information, vacancies with required skills, interview schedules, offers, and hiring decisions, and receives candidate lists with match scores, gap breakdowns, and notifications. The PESO Administrator submits employer verifications, training program and certification records, referral updates, compliance audit results, and retention outcomes, and receives analytics, referral rosters, and exported reports.
320
The level 0 diagram (Figure 2.8) shows three external entities interacting with SkillUp. The Job Applicant submits registration, profile, and skill data, applications, training enrollments, and pre-employment documents, and receives match scores, skill gap reports, training recommendations, application status, and notifications. The Employer submits company information, vacancies with required skills, interview schedules, offers, and hiring decisions, and receives candidate lists with match scores, gap breakdowns, and notifications. The PESO Administrator submits employer verifications, training program and certification records, referral updates, compliance audit results, and retention outcomes, and receives analytics, referral rosters, and exported reports.
319
The level 1 diagram (Figure 2.9) decomposes the system into the following processes: (1) Account Registration and Access, which verifies credentials and OTP and dispatches users by role; (2) Applicant Profile and Skill Management, which stores applicant profile and skill records and reads the Centralized Skills Registry; (3) Vacancy and Skill Requirement Management, which stores vacancies and their required skills; (4) Skill Matching and Gap Analysis, which reads applicant skills and vacancy requirements, computes compatibility, and writes the gap score log; (5) Application and Hiring Pipeline, which creates and updates referrals and interviews; (6) PESO Referral and Compliance Tracking, which handles pre-employment documents, start-date verification, and retention outcomes; (7) Training Recommendation, Enrollment, and Certification, which cross-references gaps with training programs, records enrollments, and marks certified skills as PESO-verified; (8) Notifications; and (9) Analytics and Reporting, which aggregates data into dashboards and CSV exports. The principal data stores are Users and Profiles, Centralized Skills, Applicant Skills, Vacancies and Skill Requirements, Referrals and Interviews, Training Programs and Enrollments, Gap Score Log, Notifications, and Job Bookmarks.
321
The level 1 diagram (Figure 2.9) decomposes the system into the following processes: (1) Account Registration and Access, which verifies credentials and OTP and dispatches users by role; (2) Applicant Profile and Skill Management, which stores applicant profile and skill records and reads the Centralized Skills Registry; (3) Vacancy and Skill Requirement Management, which stores vacancies and their required skills; (4) Skill Matching and Gap Analysis, which reads applicant skills and vacancy requirements, computes compatibility, and writes the gap score log; (5) Application and Hiring Pipeline, which creates and updates referrals and interviews; (6) PESO Referral and Compliance Tracking, which handles pre-employment documents, start-date verification, and retention outcomes; (7) Training Recommendation, Enrollment, and Certification, which cross-references gaps with training programs, records enrollments, and marks certified skills as PESO-verified; (8) Notifications; and (9) Analytics and Reporting, which aggregates data into dashboards and CSV exports. The principal data stores are Users and Profiles, Centralized Skills, Applicant Skills, Vacancies and Skill Requirements, Referrals and Interviews, Training Programs and Enrollments, Gap Score Log, Notifications, and Job Bookmarks.

Physical Specifications
324
Physical Specifications
323
Physically, SkillUp is deployed as a single Django application on one Linux virtual private server: a DigitalOcean droplet in Singapore running Ubuntu 24.04 with one vCPU, 512 MB of RAM, and 10 GB of storage. Nginx listens on port 80 as the reverse proxy, serves static and media files directly from disk, denies the encrypted identification directories, and forwards every other request to Gunicorn over a unix socket, with two workers of two threads each supervised by systemd. The application server runs Python 3.12 with Django 6, and the data tier is a local SQLite database file reached through the Django ORM, with uploaded documents held in a local media directory rather than in an object store. Outbound integration is limited to three HTTPS services: the Gemini API for the eight AI-assisted functions, the email delivery cascade, and embedded YouTube lessons. The topology has no load balancer, cache tier, message queue, or replica, which is appropriate for a demonstration and a small pilot; PostgreSQL is documented as the migration path once concurrent writes exceed what a single SQLite writer can serialise.
325
Physically, SkillUp is deployed as a single Django application on one Linux virtual private server: a DigitalOcean droplet in Singapore running Ubuntu 24.04 with one vCPU, 512 MB of RAM, and 10 GB of storage. Nginx listens on port 80 as the reverse proxy, serves static and media files directly from disk, denies the encrypted identification directories, and forwards every other request to Gunicorn over a unix socket, with two workers of two threads each supervised by systemd. The application server runs Python 3.12 with Django 6, and the data tier is a local SQLite database file reached through the Django ORM, with uploaded documents held in a local media directory rather than in an object store. Outbound integration is limited to three HTTPS services: the Gemini API for the eight AI-assisted functions, the email delivery cascade, and embedded YouTube lessons. The topology has no load balancer, cache tier, message queue, or replica, which is appropriate for a demonstration and a small pilot; PostgreSQL is documented as the migration path once concurrent writes exceed what a single SQLite writer can serialise.

System Design

System Design
325
Architectural Design
327
Architectural Design
326
SkillUp is a role-based web application built on the Django framework and can be described using a three-tier client-server structure that separates presentation, application logic, and data.
328
SkillUp is a role-based web application built on the Django framework and can be described using a three-tier client-server structure that separates presentation, application logic, and data.
327
Presentation Layer (Client Side): Semantic HTML5, CSS3, and vanilla JavaScript (ES6+) rendered in a standard web browser, with Tailwind CSS loaded from a content delivery network and charts drawn as inline SVG and CSS bars and progress rings. Django templates are segregated by role (applicant, employer, admin) so that each user sees a dedicated portal.
329
Presentation Layer (Client Side): Semantic HTML5, CSS3, and vanilla JavaScript (ES6+) rendered in a standard web browser, with Tailwind CSS loaded from a content delivery network and charts drawn as inline SVG and CSS bars and progress rings. Django templates are segregated by role (applicant, employer, admin) so that each user sees a dedicated portal.
328
Application Layer (Server Side): The Django project (`skillup_project`) holds configuration, settings, routing, and WSGI/ASGI handlers. The core `tracker` application contains the models, views, URL routes, forms, and business logic, including role dispatch after login, the matching and gap analysis computation, course mapping, the referral (hiring and compliance) workflow, notifications, and analytics.
330
Application Layer (Server Side): The Django project (`skillup_project`) holds configuration, settings, routing, and WSGI/ASGI handlers. The core `tracker` application contains the models, views, URL routes, forms, and business logic, including role dispatch after login, the matching and gap analysis computation, course mapping, the referral (hiring and compliance) workflow, notifications, and analytics.
329
Data Layer: A relational database (SQLite) accessed through the Django ORM, together with a `media` store for candidate documents, résumés, certifications, pre-employment files, and logos.
331
Data Layer: A relational database (SQLite) accessed through the Django ORM, together with a `media` store for candidate documents, résumés, certifications, pre-employment files, and logos.
330
User authentication uses the Django authentication system extended with custom profile models and OTP verification. The project also includes maintenance utilities: a seed script that populates sample applicants, employers, skills, vacancies, and courses; a script that resets transactional data for demonstrations; and a system check script that verifies models, routes, and database integrity.
332
User authentication uses the Django authentication system extended with custom profile models and OTP verification. The project also includes maintenance utilities: a seed script that populates sample applicants, employers, skills, vacancies, and courses; a script that resets transactional data for demonstrations; and a system check script that verifies models, routes, and database integrity.

The database consists of normalized relational entities grouped into six domains: identity and profile; skills taxonomy; jobs and matching; placement and engagement; learning; and system and legacy configuration. The 22 models and their documented attributes are summarized below, and the relationships follow from the implemented Django schema.

Process Flows
358
Process Flows
357
Figure 2.10 ERD
359
Figure 2.10. ERD
358
The central workflow of SkillUp proceeds through the following stages, which also define the order in which the modules are used.
360
The central workflow of SkillUp proceeds through the following stages, which also define the order in which the modules are used.
359
Registration, verification, and role dispatch. A user registers by selecting a role; the system creates an account and profile and a six-digit OTP; the user verifies the account and logs in, after which the user is routed to the applicant, employer, or PESO administrator dashboard.
361
Registration, verification, and role dispatch. A user registers by selecting a role; the system creates an account and profile and a six-digit OTP; the user verifies the account and logs in, after which the user is routed to the applicant, employer, or PESO administrator dashboard.
360
Applicant profile and skills. The applicant completes the profile wizard, adds skills from the registry, and uploads a résumé and credentials. Skills are recorded as self-reported until verified.
362
Applicant profile and skills. The applicant completes the profile wizard, adds skills from the registry, and uploads a résumé and credentials. Skills are recorded as self-reported until verified.
361
Employer registration and vacancy creation. The employer registers a company, is subject to verification by a PESO administrator, and creates a vacancy with one or more required skills drawn from the Centralized Skills Registry. Publishing activates the matching engine.
363
Employer registration and vacancy creation. The employer registers a company, is subject to verification by a PESO administrator, and creates a vacancy with one or more required skills drawn from the Centralized Skills Registry. Publishing activates the matching engine.
362
Matching and skill gap analysis. The engine compares applicant skills with vacancy requirements, computes a composite compatibility percentage weighted at 0.60 for skills, 0.20 for education and 0.20 for experience with a career-alignment adjustment, and classifies each required skill as an exact, transferable or missing match. Missing skills are cross-referenced with training programs.
364
Matching and skill gap analysis. The engine compares applicant skills with vacancy requirements, computes a composite compatibility percentage weighted at 0.60 for skills, 0.20 for education and 0.20 for experience with a career-alignment adjustment, and classifies each required skill as an exact, transferable or missing match. Missing skills are cross-referenced with training programs.
363
Job discovery and application. The applicant browses vacancies sorted by compatibility and applies; a referral record is created with pending status and the employer and PESO coordinators are notified.
365
Job discovery and application. The applicant browses vacancies sorted by compatibility and applies; a referral record is created with pending status and the employer and PESO coordinators are notified.
364
Employer review, interview, and hiring. The employer reviews candidates with match scores and gap breakdowns, schedules an interview, and extends an offer; the applicant accepts; the employer marks the applicant as hired (probationary or regular) and the vacancy’s remaining slots decrease.
366
Employer review, interview, and hiring. The employer reviews candidates with match scores and gap breakdowns, schedules an interview, and extends an offer; the applicant accepts; the employer marks the applicant as hired (probationary or regular) and the vacancy’s remaining slots decrease.
365
PESO referral and pre-employment compliance. The applicant uploads pre-employment documents; the PESO coordinator audits each and marks it complete, then verifies the actual start date.
367
PESO referral and pre-employment compliance. The applicant uploads pre-employment documents; the PESO coordinator audits each and marks it complete, then verifies the actual start date.
366
Training recommendation, enrollment, and certification. Applicants who are not hired, or who wish to upskill, view courses mapped to their gaps and enroll; on passing the capstone examination the system records the trained skill as training-verified evidence and issues a certificate carrying a public verification code.
368
Training recommendation, enrollment, and certification. Applicants who are not hired, or who wish to upskill, view courses mapped to their gaps and enroll; on passing the capstone examination the system records the trained skill as training-verified evidence and issues a certificate carrying a public verification code.
367
Post-placement monitoring. PESO records report-for-work confirmation, the mid-probation outcome and the final probation outcome, can report over 30-, 60-, 90- and 365-day age-of-referral windows, and can export compliance reports.
369
Post-placement monitoring. PESO records report-for-work confirmation, the mid-probation outcome and the final probation outcome, can report over 30-, 60-, 90- and 365-day age-of-referral windows, and can export compliance reports.

User Interface Design
376
User Interface Design
375
Each role has a dedicated portal. The screens are grouped by role below; captioned screenshots of the implemented screens follow the description.
377
Each role has a dedicated portal. The screens are grouped by role below; captioned screenshots of the implemented screens follow the description.
376
Common screens. The Login and Signup screens authenticate users and register them with a selected role; the Verification screen accepts the six-digit OTP and supports resending the code; the Forgot Password and Reset Password screens support account recovery.
378
Common screens. The Login and Signup screens authenticate users and register them with a selected role; the Verification screen accepts the six-digit OTP and supports resending the code; the Forgot Password and Reset Password screens support account recovery.
377
Applicant portal. The Applicant Dashboard summarizes the applicant’s activity. The Profile and Onboarding Wizard screens manage personal, education, work, career preference, skill, and document data. The Job Search screen lists vacancies sorted by compatibility. The Skill Gap screen shows the met and missing breakdown against a vacancy. The Applications screen tracks application status and hosts pre-employment document upload. The Training screen lists recommended courses and enrollment. The Notifications and Settings screens complete the portal.
379
Applicant portal. The Applicant Dashboard summarizes the applicant’s activity. The Profile and Onboarding Wizard screens manage personal, education, work, career preference, skill, and document data. The Job Search screen lists vacancies sorted by compatibility. The Skill Gap screen shows the met and missing breakdown against a vacancy. The Applications screen tracks application status and hosts pre-employment document upload. The Training screen lists recommended courses and enrollment. The Notifications and Settings screens complete the portal.
378
Employer portal. The Employer Dashboard summarizes recruitment activity. Job Management supports vacancy creation, editing, skill mapping, open/close, and deletion. The Applicants screen shows the candidate pool with match scores, and the Applicant Detail screen shows a candidate’s profile, résumé, and radar gap chart. Gap Analysis provides a batch view for active openings. The Hiring Tracker is a Kanban pipeline, with screens for scheduling and cancelling interviews and sending offers. Company Profile and Notifications complete the portal.
380
Employer portal. The Employer Dashboard summarizes recruitment activity. Job Management supports vacancy creation, editing, skill mapping, open/close, and deletion. The Applicants screen shows the candidate pool with match scores, and the Applicant Detail screen shows a candidate’s profile, résumé, and radar gap chart. Gap Analysis provides a batch view for active openings. The Hiring Tracker is a Kanban pipeline, with screens for scheduling and cancelling interviews and sending offers. Company Profile and Notifications complete the portal.
379
PESO administrator portal. The Executive Dashboard presents analytics. Screens for the Applicant Directory (with match inspector, coordinator interview, status update, and CSV import), Employer Directory (verification), Vacancies (referral rosters), Training Monitoring (course details, roster, and certification), Skill Monitoring (supply and demand heatmap), Job Matching analytics, Employment Tracking (onboarding, compliance, and 30/60/90-day retention), Referral management (creation, status update, contact logging), and CSV export support coordination and reporting.
381
PESO administrator portal. The Executive Dashboard presents analytics. Screens for the Applicant Directory (with match inspector, coordinator interview, status update, and CSV import), Employer Directory (verification), Vacancies (referral rosters), Training Monitoring (course details, roster, and certification), Skill Monitoring (supply and demand heatmap), Job Matching analytics, Employment Tracking (onboarding, compliance, and 30/60/90-day retention), Referral management (creation, status update, contact logging), and CSV export support coordination and reporting.

System Implementation
421
System Implementation
420
Deployment Strategy
422
Deployment Strategy
421
SkillUp is deployed as a web-based application that users access through a web browser. Deployment involves installing the Django application, configuring the relational database, and loading initial data (skills registry, training catalog, and user accounts). The project's seed script can populate sample applicants, employers, skills, vacancies, and courses, and its reset script returns transactional data to a clean state for demonstrations. The deployment environment for the evaluation is a DigitalOcean droplet (Linux virtual private server), running Nginx as the reverse proxy and Gunicorn as the WSGI application server, with a local SQLite database.
423
SkillUp is deployed as a web-based application that users access through a web browser. Deployment involves installing the Django application, configuring the relational database, and loading initial data (skills registry, training catalog, and user accounts). The project's seed script can populate sample applicants, employers, skills, vacancies, and courses, and its reset script returns transactional data to a clean state for demonstrations. The deployment environment for the evaluation is a DigitalOcean droplet (Linux virtual private server), running Nginx as the reverse proxy and Gunicorn as the WSGI application server, with a local SQLite database.
422
User Orientation and Training
424
User Orientation and Training
423
After deployment, users receive hands-on orientation in the features relevant to their role. Applicants are oriented in registering and verifying an account, building a profile, adding skills, viewing match scores and skill gaps, applying and tracking applications, and viewing and enrolling in training. Employers are oriented in registering a company, creating vacancies with required skills, reviewing candidates and their gap analysis, scheduling interviews, and using the hiring tracker. PESO administrators are oriented in verifying employers, monitoring applicants and vacancies, managing training and certification, tracking employment compliance and retention, and exporting reports.
425
After deployment, users receive hands-on orientation in the features relevant to their role. Applicants are oriented in registering and verifying an account, building a profile, adding skills, viewing match scores and skill gaps, applying and tracking applications, and viewing and enrolling in training. Employers are oriented in registering a company, creating vacancies with required skills, reviewing candidates and their gap analysis, scheduling interviews, and using the hiring tracker. PESO administrators are oriented in verifying employers, monitoring applicants and vacancies, managing training and certification, tracking employment compliance and retention, and exporting reports.

Software Development Tools
443
Software Development Tools
442
Website Development
444
Website Development
443
SkillUp was developed using Python and the Django web framework. The front end uses Semantic HTML5, CSS3, and vanilla JavaScript (ES6+), with Tailwind CSS loaded from a content delivery network and charts rendered as inline SVG and CSS bars and progress rings. The Django project is organized into the `skillup_project` configuration and the `tracker` application, whose templates are separated by role. Uploaded résumés, credentials, pre-employment files, and logos are stored in a media directory.
445
SkillUp was developed using Python and the Django web framework. The front end uses Semantic HTML5, CSS3, and vanilla JavaScript (ES6+), with Tailwind CSS loaded from a content delivery network and charts rendered as inline SVG and CSS bars and progress rings. The Django project is organized into the `skillup_project` configuration and the `tracker` application, whose templates are separated by role. Uploaded résumés, credentials, pre-employment files, and logos are stored in a media directory.

Schedule Timeline
450
Schedule Timeline
449
The project followed the five phases described under System Development Methodology, and each phase produced the artifact on which the next depended. Requirements analysis and planning came first, producing the functional and non-functional requirements in Tables 7 to 9 and the process and data models in Figures 2.8 to 2.11. System design followed, fixing the architecture, the data model and the role interfaces described in this chapter. Development then implemented the sixteen modules, and testing ran alongside development rather than after it, so that defects were corrected before the next iteration began. Implementation and evaluation came last: the system was deployed to the DigitalOcean host, the five IT experts were oriented on the three role interfaces, and the evaluation questionnaire was administered over one week. Table 14 sets out the deployment and evaluation activities with the personnel assigned to each and their expected duration. The schedule was tracked against that table rather than against fixed calendar dates, because the evaluation window depended on the availability of the expert respondents. The estimated position of each phase across the two semesters is shown in Figure 2.11.
451
The project followed the five phases described under System Development Methodology, and each phase produced the artifact on which the next depended. Requirements analysis and planning came first, producing the functional and non-functional requirements in Tables 2.4 to 2.6 and the process and data models in Figures 2.8 to 2.11. System design followed, fixing the architecture, the data model and the role interfaces described in this chapter. Development then implemented the sixteen modules, and testing ran alongside development rather than after it, so that defects were corrected before the next iteration began. Installation and Configuration
471
Installation and Configuration
470
The platform is installed on the host described under Physical Specifications. The application directory is created, a virtual environment is prepared, the dependencies in the requirements file are installed, migrations are applied to the database, and static files are collected. The environment variables for the secret key, the allowed hosts, the database, and the optional Gemini and email credentials are set before the first start, and the demonstration data are seeded only after the superuser account has been created.
472

The environment variables for the secret key, the allowed hosts, the database, and the optional Gemini and email credentials are set before the first start, and the demonstration data are seeded only after the superuser account has been created.
471
User Orientation
473
User Orientation
472
Orientation is conducted separately for each role, as described under User Orientation and Training. Applicants are walked through profile completion, skill evidence, job search, gap inspection, and enrolment; employers through vacancy creation with required skills, candidate review, interview scheduling, and offers; and PESO administrators through employer verification, referral creation, training authoring, compliance auditing, and the export of reports.

Pilot Implementation
475
Pilot Implementation
474
The pilot runs with the partner PESO office and a small set of employers, using seeded or imported records so that ranking, referral, and training flows can be exercised without exposing real applicant documents. Pilot findings are logged as defects or as configuration changes rather than counted as evaluation results, so that the evaluation reported in Chapter 3 remains the record of the system as it stood at assessment.
476

Pilot findings are logged as defects or as configuration changes rather than counted as evaluation results, so that the evaluation reported in Chapter 3 remains the record of the system as it stood at assessment.
475
Monitoring and Evaluation
477
Monitoring and Evaluation
476
Monitoring uses the employment-tracking and analytics views: conversion and no-response rates, stale referrals, the placement funnel, and the skill supply and demand tables. Evaluation follows the design described in this chapter, with IT experts rating the platform against the nine ISO/IEC 25010:2023 characteristics after examining all three role interfaces.

477
Maintenance
479
Maintenance
478
Maintenance is scheduled rather than continuous. It covers dependency updates, database backup and a periodic restore drill, review of the skill taxonomy against the vacancies employers actually post, and review of the AI-generated curricula that PESO publishes. Every change to the scoring configuration is recorded in the audit trail so that a change in ranking can be traced to the setting that caused it.
480

Verification, Validation and Testing
