- CHAPTER 1
1
+ CHAPTER 1 — PROJECT RATIONALE
2
- PROJECT RATIONALE
2
Introduction
3
“Am I ready for this job?” is a question every job seeker asks, and today the answer depends on skills that are changing faster than many people can track. The World Economic Forum (2025) reports that employers expect 39% of workers’ core skills to change by 2030, and Braun et al. (2024b), in a review of 40 articles, argued that upskilling existing workers is needed because recruiting young talent alone cannot close skill gaps. As graduating IT students who will soon enter this labor market, the researchers see this problem as close to home.
4
The mismatch is also visible in the Philippines, where Melchor (2022) found that about 39% of employed Filipinos are overeducated and over a quarter are undereducated. Job seekers often apply without knowing how well their skills fit, while employers screen unstructured résumés without a consistent basis for comparison. When both describe skills against the same taxonomy, the gaps become visible, and training can then close them (Busso et al., 2022; Algarni & Sheldon, 2023; Thammarak et al., 2024).
5
However, course recommendation research is concentrated in academic settings (Algarni & Sheldon, 2023; Thammarak et al., 2024), and HR analytics research focuses on internal HR functions rather than skills-based training alignment (Cayrat & Boxall, 2022). No reviewed study integrates skills matching, gap identification, hiring, and training recommendation in one platform that also supports a public employment office.
6
This study therefore designs, develops, and evaluates SkillUp, a web-based platform that uses skills as the common language between applicants and vacancies. It scores each applicant–vacancy match, shows which required skills are met or missing, supports hiring, and maps gaps to training programs, with a Public Employment Service Office (PESO) administrator role to verify employers and monitor placement and retention. Grounded in Human Capital Theory (Becker, 1964) and assessed against ISO/IEC 25010:2023, the study contributes a tested model of skills-based matching. It reflects the DYCI core value of Magis, the pursuit of more for the common good, by helping people keep refining their skills. It also supports SDG 4, SDG 8, and SDG 9 (United Nations, 2015).
7
Review of Related Literature and Studies
8
This review is organized around the problem the study addresses: connecting job applicants with employer requirements through skills data, identifying skill gaps, and directing applicants to relevant training. Literature on human resource analytics is limited to recruitment and talent matching, where it informs the employer-side and PESO-side functions of the platform. Because SkillUp also uses generative AI to extract fields from documents and to draft training content, the review includes work on AI-generated learning content and on the privacy and fairness safeguards that AI systems in hiring require. Sources are drawn mainly from 2019 onward, with older foundational work retained only where it anchors a theory or a method.
9
Related Literature
10
Skills Gap, Employability and Labour-Market Alignment
11
The literature converges on a single premise: qualifications no longer stand in for capability, so the unit of comparison between a person and a job has to be the skill itself. The World Economic Forum (2025) estimates that employers expect 39% of workers' core skills to change by 2030 and that 59 of every 100 workers will need training by then, which makes continuous reskilling a structural condition rather than a remedial one. Braun et al. (2024b), reviewing 40 articles, conclude that recruiting young talent cannot close skill gaps on its own and that upskilling the existing workforce is required, supported by flexible training and coordination among employers, education providers and job seekers. Tushar and Sooraksa (2023), synthesising employability research, show that employers judge readiness on specific skills rather than on credentials. Taken together, these findings justify a design decision that SkillUp makes at the schema level: skills are stored as rows linked to a controlled vocabulary, not as free text inside a résumé, because only normalised skills can be compared, counted and mapped to training.
12
In the Philippine setting the problem has a second dimension, which is definitional. Melchor (2022), using national labour data, finds that about 39% of employed Filipinos are overeducated and more than a quarter are undereducated, and that a surplus year of schooling returns only about 5% in wages against 7% to 19% for a year that matches the job. Monteverde et al. (2025) show why mismatch persists: TESDA, CHED and the Philippine Statistics Authority classify middle skills differently, so training programs are built on inconsistent competency standards and curricula drift from the priority sectors named in the Philippine Development Plan. For a platform, the practical implication is that a shared taxonomy is not a convenience but a precondition; SkillUp's 789-skill registry and its 20-code vacancy category taxonomy are an attempt to supply one locally, and the finding also predicts the registry's main weakness, which is that a curated vocabulary must be maintained against employer demand or it will lag it.
13
Public employment services are the institutions positioned to act on this. The OECD (2022) documents how the pandemic accelerated digitalisation in public employment services and how member countries use digital tools to connect people with jobs, while stressing that the performance of those tools must be monitored and evaluated rather than assumed. That framing matches the division of labour in SkillUp, where the platform performs the matching and the PESO administrator owns verification, referral, training authoring and outcome reporting.
14
Data-Driven Recruitment, Fairness and the Limits of Self-Report
15
Research on algorithmic hiring has moved from whether matching is possible to how it should be governed. Qin et al. (2023) survey artificial intelligence techniques for talent analytics across talent management, organisation management and labour-market analysis, and Mashayekhi et al. (2022) survey e-recruitment recommenders around eight practical challenges, of which data quality, cold start and fairness are the most consequential for a small system. Tang et al. (2025), reviewing 85 studies on explainable person-job recommendation, report a persistent trade-off between recommendation performance and explainability. For SkillUp, that trade-off is resolved by refusing it: the composite score is a fixed weighted formula that can be decomposed for any user, and the language model is confined to explaining a result it did not produce. This is a deliberate choice of lower expressive power in exchange for auditability, and it is consistent with what the recommender literature reports about the cost of opacity.
16
Fairness and privacy impose hard constraints on what a score may read. Fabris et al. (2024) survey bias in algorithmic hiring across technical, legal and social perspectives, finding that bias can enter through institutional practice, candidate preference and technical blind spots, that the effects compound for intersectional groups, and that mitigation results are uneven enough to require context-specific measurement. In the Philippines, the National Privacy Commission (2024) requires transparency about how an automated system works, accountability for its outcomes, meaningful human intervention in significant decisions, limits on bias, accuracy of the personal data used, and data minimisation. SkillUp's response is architectural: the scoring function reads only skills, education, experience, certifications and stated preferences, and the system document records that age, sex, civil status, address, photograph, disability, name and nationality are excluded by design. That exclusion is a genuine safeguard, though it is a narrow one, since the curated transferable-skill groups still encode the authors' assumptions and no demographic bias evaluation has been run.
17
The quality of the skills data is the remaining weak point. Adamecz et al. (2025), analysing 4,429 individuals in the British Cohort Study 1970, find that self-assessments track objective ability unevenly across ability levels and that men tend to overestimate and women to underestimate their ability. The finding is about self-assessment generally rather than about job applicants, but it is the strongest available argument against building a matching engine on a self-rated scale, and it is why SkillUp records how each skill was obtained instead of how well the applicant believes they perform it. The same reasoning explains the system's most important limitation: provenance is a coarse signal, and a skill acquired once and a skill used for years carry the same weight.
18
Recommendation, Training and the Evidence on Retraining
19
Course recommendation research supplies the closest technical precedent for gap-driven training advice, and it also explains why most such systems do not transfer directly to an employment service. Algarni and Sheldon (2023), reviewing 35 studies, identify large and changing decision spaces and the cold-start problem for new users as the dominant difficulties. Frej et al. (2024) argue that course recommenders should account for labour-market demand, observing that courses are typically recommended from learner interest and past behaviour without reference to the skills employers actually ask for. SkillUp's training module takes the route both papers point toward and neither implements in an employment setting: a recommendation is derived from the intersection between a specific vacancy's unmet requirements and the catalogue's addressed skills, so it requires no interaction history and is therefore immune to cold start by construction.
20
The evidence on whether such training works is encouraging but conditional. Busso et al. (2022), in a meta-analysis of 40 retraining programs for unemployed workers, find an average gain of about 2.6 percentage points in job-finding and about 0.08 of a standard deviation in wages, with stronger results for programs that teach technical skills, combine classroom instruction with on-the-job training, or are run with private-sector partners. Humlum et al. (2023), exploiting quasi-random assignment of unemployed workers to caseworkers in Denmark, find that classroom training raised employment by roughly 25% two years after job loss, with gains operating through completion and occupational switching. The gap between a modest average and a large conditional effect is the argument for targeting: outcomes depend on whether the training fits the person and the local labour market, which is exactly what mapping a missing skill to a specific course is meant to achieve.
21
Targeting also has a cautionary literature. Eppel et al. (2025) examine statistical profiling as a tool for allocating active labour-market measures in Austria and find that shifting resources from low-chance to medium-chance job seekers would not deliver the expected efficiency gain, and that training was not reliably less effective for job seekers with weak prospects. The implication for SkillUp is that the gap list should drive the offer of training without being used to screen anyone out of it, which is how the platform is built: a low composite score produces a gap report and a training recommendation, never an exclusion.
22
Generative AI has entered this space as a production tool for content rather than as a recommender. Yan et al. (2024) review the promises and challenges of generative AI for human learning, noting gains in personalised support and material diversity alongside model imperfection, ethical problems and disruption of assessment. SkillUp's use is deliberately narrow: the model drafts a curriculum skeleton and reading content that a PESO administrator then publishes, edits or discards. The evaluation literature reviewed in the next section suggests that this division of labour is the right one, while the system's own record shows that the review step is available but not enforced.
23
Evaluating the Artefact
24
Because the study evaluates a software artefact as well as a mechanism, the choice of quality model needs justification rather than assertion. Gobov and Zuieva (2025) compare McCall, Boehm, FURPS and the ISO SQuaRE family and conclude that ISO/IEC 25010:2023 offers the most comprehensive structure, treating functional suitability and compatibility as essential; their survey of 328 analysts also finds that documentation of maintainability, compatibility and portability lags behind usability, security and performance. Prameswari and Setyawan (2025) combine ISO/IEC 25010 technical characteristics with SERVQUAL user-perception dimensions and report that most dimensions, including functional suitability, usability, reliability and security, were rated very adequate, with usability and reliability significantly influencing user experience. Together these support the instrument described in Chapter 2: end-users rate the characteristics they can experience, IT experts rate the full model, and the analysis is descriptive and disaggregated by respondent group.
25
Synthesis
26
Four conclusions follow from the review and shape the design under study. First, employability is assessed on specific skills, and in the Philippines the vocabulary used to name those skills is itself fragmented, so a maintained taxonomy is the first requirement of any matching system (World Economic Forum, 2025; Tushar & Sooraksa, 2023; Melchor, 2022; Monteverde et al., 2025). Second, matching should be explainable and should not read protected attributes, which argues for a deterministic weighted score over a learned ranker and for recording how each skill was obtained rather than how well the applicant rates themselves (Tang et al., 2025; Fabris et al., 2024; National Privacy Commission, 2024; Adamecz et al., 2025). Third, training advice is more likely to change outcomes when it is tied to a specific unmet requirement than when it follows learner interest, and the retraining evidence supports targeting without screening people out (Algarni & Sheldon, 2023; Frej et al., 2024; Busso et al., 2022; Humlum et al., 2023; Eppel et al., 2025). Fourth, a system of this kind should be judged against a recognised quality model with both expert and user raters (Gobov & Zuieva, 2025; Prameswari & Setyawan, 2025).
27
Related Studies
28
Philippine Employment Systems and the PESO Context
29
The closest local precedent is a PESO-facing matching system rather than a commercial job board. Ycoy et al. (2022) designed and built PESMATCH, a web application for the Tagum City Public Employment Service Office that applies the Gale-Shapley algorithm to pair job seekers with hiring companies on stated preferences and qualifications, reporting a percentage match and a recommended position. The study establishes that a municipal employment office will adopt a matching tool and that a percentage is an intelligible output for its staff. It also marks the boundary SkillUp tries to cross: PESMATCH matches on preference and qualification as declared, and does not represent required skills as a normalised vacancy attribute, does not diagnose which required skills are missing, and does not connect a gap to a training program. The national counterpart is the PhilJobNet platform and the PESO Employment Information System, governed by Department of Labor and Employment Order No. 165-16 (Department of Labor and Employment, 2016), which sets out how PESO offices register vacancies, refer applicants and report placements. That order is the operational context a PESO-built system must fit, and it is the reason SkillUp treats the referral, not the application, as the unit of record.
30
The weaknesses of the existing infrastructure are documented. Bayudan-Dacuycuy et al. (2024), reviewing the inputs to the Philippine Labor Market Information System for TESDA, find that skills mismatch has persisted for decades, that PhilJobNet is the most accessible source of online vacancy data but covers only formally registered firms, and that the Philippine Skills Framework and TESDA's skills map do not share a common taxonomy. They also report that PESO coverage and service quality are limited and that impact monitoring is absent. Those four findings map directly onto four SkillUp design choices: a single internal taxonomy, vacancy records with normalised requirements, PESO-side analytics, and outcome tracking that extends to probation and separation. TESDA's Study on the Employment of TVET Graduates is the closest national instrument to a training-to-employment tracer and reports the employment outcomes and skills utilisation of TVET graduates (Technical Education and Skills Development Authority, 2023); it supplies the outcome categories that a municipal system should be able to reproduce for its own completers.
31
Prior Systems for Skill-Gap Matching and Training Recommendation
32
Two studies come close to SkillUp's mechanism without reaching an employment service. Hien et al. (2024) propose a knowledge-based, rule-driven course recommender for information technology careers that derives recommendations from a learner's identified skill gaps and career goals, and report acceptability in a small user survey. Braun et al. (2024a) describe a tool built for a Swedish upskilling programme that identifies an individual's skill gaps and matches them to training modules. Both validate the gap-to-course mechanism, and both stop short of the step that makes SkillUp's loop consequential: neither draws its requirements from live vacancies posted by employers in an employment service, so neither can show that the skill a person is trained for is the skill a specific local employer asked for. That difference is also a weakness, since a vacancy-derived gap is only as good as the employer's specification of the vacancy, and the system has no calibration of employer reliability.
33
Language-Model Extraction and Its Measured Limits
34
The extraction tasks SkillUp delegates to a language model have been benchmarked, and the results argue for assistance rather than automation. De Quadros et al. (2025) compare large language models on résumé information extraction in zero-shot and one-shot settings, find Gemini 2.5 Pro the most accurate, and report that sparsely filled résumé sections substantially reduce average accuracy. That finding is directly operational: SkillUp parses uploaded PDF, DOCX and text files, and scanned PDFs without a text layer yield little text and no OCR fallback, so extraction quality will vary with document quality in exactly the way the benchmark predicts. The design response is to pre-fill rather than overwrite and to leave every parsed field editable, which is what the system does.
35
Hiring-specific benchmarks are more cautionary. Anzenberg et al. (2025) compare general-purpose language models with a supervised hiring model on roughly 10,000 real candidate-job pairs and find the supervised model both more accurate and more equitable across gender and race groups, warning against deploying off-the-shelf models in hiring without safeguards; the authors are affiliated with the vendor of the supervised model, so the result should be read with that affiliation in view. SkillUp's architecture already reflects the warning, in that the model never sets a score, but the paper should say so explicitly rather than leave it to be discovered.
36
Generative AI in Curriculum Development
37
The curriculum-generation literature is consistent about capability and equally consistent about the need for expert review, which makes the review step the finding that matters. Bektik et al. (2024) report an Open University trial in which GPT-4 with retrieval condensed an existing module into a continuing professional development course; no factual errors were found, but the authors conclude that subject experts remain essential. Lin et al. (2025) use GPT-4o with retrieval to build scenario-based lessons for training novice mathematics tutors and find that lessons generated in three segments received the highest human ratings, while evaluators also noted generic feedback, unclear instructions and fabricated references. Reihanian et al. (2025), reviewing 52 papers on generative AI in computer science education, report efficiency gains alongside hallucinations, error propagation and bias, and recommend hybrid approaches that pair generation with human evaluation. Harvey et al. (2025), interviewing six education-technology providers and 23 educators, find the two groups frame harm differently, with providers emphasising technical failures and educators emphasising effects on learning, workload and autonomy. Nguyen et al. (2026) propose a five-stage framework for rapid upskilling in which AI produces content and humans retain review roles, and report mixed results that include 95% accuracy on a 20-example sample alongside only 5 of 10 chapters meeting a progression target. Yao et al. (2026) present a multi-agent system that produces course materials to reduce faculty workload, extending the same division of labour to instructional design.
38
For SkillUp the practical reading is narrow and uncomfortable. The evidence supports generating a draft and having a person with subject expertise approve it. The system makes that possible, since PESO authors, edits and publishes curricula in a visual builder, and the AI output is stored as editable JSON. But the system does not require approval before publication, and its own documentation states that generated curricula can be published without a review step. The paper must therefore describe the review as a procedural control available to PESO, not as an enforced gate, or the claim will not survive a demonstration.
39
Two policy documents set the governance expectation for this kind of use. The OECD (2026) reviews how artificial intelligence is beginning to be used in developing vocational education and training curricula, qualifications and occupational standards, finding opportunities to make training more relevant and efficient while stressing the need for governance and a human-centred approach. Dwyer (2026), in an ISS Institute fellowship report, identifies factors for using generative AI effectively in vocational curriculum and resource development, including human-AI collaboration, adherence to vocational design principles and strict ethical oversight. Both are consistent with a PESO-reviewed drafting workflow and both would be cited against a claim that generated content can be published unreviewed.
40
Certification and the Value of a Verifiable Credential
41
The training loop only pays off if the resulting credential means something to an employer. Hossain et al. (2024) argue, from a literature review, that online learning and certification platforms allow people to acquire in-demand technical skills more cheaply and quickly than traditional routes and that the resulting certificates can improve job prospects for unemployed people. The argument depends on the certificate being checkable, which is why SkillUp exposes an unauthenticated verification page and prints a QR code pointing to it. The same literature supplies the caveat the paper should carry: a code proves a record exists, not that the person presenting it is the record's holder, and SkillUp's short code with no rate limit is a verification convenience rather than an integrity control.
42
Synthesis of Studies
43
The reviewed Philippine studies establish the need for an integrated employment-matching system and identify existing gaps in local employment services. These studies demonstrate the willingness of municipal offices to adopt matching tools (Ycoy et al., 2022), the established regulatory framework for referral processes (Department of Labor and Employment, 2016), limitations in the completeness and compatibility of national employment information systems (Bayudan-Dacuycuy et al., 2024), and the need for more localized graduate employment outcome monitoring (Technical Education and Skills Development Authority, 2023).
44
Meanwhile, international studies demonstrate the feasibility of skills-gap-based recommendation systems and the potential of automated information extraction and generation, while also recognizing their limitations (Hien et al., 2024; Braun et al., 2024a; de Quadros et al., 2025; Anzenberg et al., 2025; Bektik et al., 2024; Lin et al., 2025). These studies further emphasize the importance of expert review and validation to ensure the reliability and appropriateness of generated recommendations (Reihanian et al., 2025; Harvey et al., 2025; Nguyen et al., 2026; OECD, 2026; Dwyer, 2026).
45
Despite these contributions, the studies reviewed do not demonstrate the complete integration of vacancy skill requirements, applicant skill profiles with documented sources, deterministic skills-gap analysis, gap-based training recommendations, certification-based skill updates, and employment placement monitoring with probation and retention outcomes in a unified record. Addressing this integration gap, the present study proposes a system coordinated by a Public Employment Service Office (PESO) administrator. Its software quality will be evaluated using ISO/IEC 25010:2023. This integration constitutes the primary contribution of the proposed study.
46
Statement of the Problem
47
Job applicants and employers lack an efficient, structured way to match applicant competencies with vacancy requirements. Applicants cannot readily see how their skills compare with what a vacancy demands or what they would need to learn to qualify, and employers cannot readily compare candidates against the skills a vacancy requires. Skill deficiencies are seldom translated into concrete pathways for improvement. This capstone project addresses these limitations by developing an integrated platform that uses skills data to match applicants with vacancies, identifies skill gaps, supports the application and hiring process, and recommends relevant training.
48
General Problem
49
How can an integrated employment and talent management platform utilize structured skills data, automated gap analysis, native e-learning, and municipal oversight to connect job applicants with employer vacancies and resolve workforce skill shortages?
50
Specific Problems
51
How can applicant profiles, qualifications, and competencies be systematically organized and validated without relying on subjective, uncalibrated self-ratings?
52
How can employers systematically define job vacancies with mandatory benchmark skill requirements?
53
How can candidate competencies be compared against vacancy requirements to compute deterministic compatibility scores and identify missing skills?
54
How can artificial intelligence assist in automated résumé parsing and profile pre-fill and qualitative candidate fit reasoning without introducing algorithmic hiring bias?
55
How can identified skill gaps be dynamically mapped to native interactive training modules with automated capstone assessments?
56
How can the system issue publicly verifiable certificates that mark the related skills as training-verified upon course completion?
57
How can PESO administrators be supported in employer verification, talent bench oversight, pre-employment compliance auditing, and 30/60/90-day retention tracking?
59
- How can the platform's user acceptance and software quality (ISO/IEC 25020:2023) be empirically evaluated?
58
+ How can the platform's user acceptance and software quality (ISO/IEC 25010:2023) be empirically evaluated?
59
Objectives of the Study
60
General Objective
61
The study aims to design, develop, and evaluate an integrated employment and talent management platform that utilizes structured skills data, automated gap analysis, interactive training, and municipal coordination to connect job applicants with employer job requirements, and to assess its user acceptance and software quality.
62
Specific Objectives
63
To develop a centralized applicant profile and skills management system that organizes qualifications and validates competencies through diagnostic assessments, completed training, and verified credentials.
64
To enable employers to create company profiles and post vacancies with three to ten mandatory benchmark skills and required proficiency levels.
65
To implement a deterministic skill-matching algorithm that calculates candidate compatibility scores and identifies skill gaps through an interactive 2D radar visualizer.
66
To integrate an AI-assisted module for résumé parsing, applicant profile pre-filling, and qualitative candidate-fit analysis with safeguards against hiring bias.
67
To develop a training module that maps identified skill gaps to relevant courses and validates acquired competencies through automated capstone assessments.
68
To implement a digital certification system that issues publicly verifiable certificates and marks completed skills as training verified.
69
To develop a PESO administrative module for employer verification, talent bench management, compliance auditing, and 30/60/90-day retention monitoring.
71
- To evaluate the platform's user acceptance using the Technology Acceptance Model (TAM) and assess its software quality based on the ISO/IEC 25020:2023 standard.
70
+ To evaluate the platform's user acceptance using the Technology Acceptance Model (TAM) and assess its software quality based on the ISO/IEC 25010:2023 standard.
71
Scope and Delimitation
72
Scope
73
SkillUp is a web-based employment facilitation, skills matching, and workforce upskilling platform. It serves three primary user groups: Job Applicants (Job Seekers and Trainees), Employers (Enterprises and Hiring Managers), and PESO Administrators (Employment Officers and Coordinators). The platform is built as a Django application comprising 22 data models, 92 routes, and 50 role-partitioned templates, and it is organised into 16 functional modules. Each module is described below in terms of the role it serves, its purpose, its main functions, and its inputs and outputs.
74
Account Registration, Verification, and Access Module
75
Serves applicants and employers through public signup, and PESO staff through provisioned accounts. Its purpose is to create a validated account and dispatch the user to the correct portal. It restricts public signup to the applicant and employer roles through an allow-list, issues a six-digit one-time password by email with a resend option and a three-provider delivery fallback, offers an optional Philippine identification scan whose extracted name is compared with the name entered, and provides session-based password recovery and role guards on every protected view. Its inputs are name, email, password, role, company name for employers, an optional phone number and an optional identification image; its outputs are the user and profile records, an encrypted identification file, a verification flag, an authenticated session, and a redirect to /applicant-dashboard/, /employer-dashboard/, or /admin-dashboard/.
76
Applicant Profile, Education, Work History, Document Vault, and Résumé Ingestion Module
77
Serves the applicant. Its purpose is to assemble the record that matching later reads, with as little manual entry as possible. It provides a step-by-step wizard and separate education, dated work experience and certification sub-records, recalculates total experience on every work-history change, and stores résumés, diplomas and credentials in a document vault. A résumé-parsing function extracts text from PDF, DOCX and TXT files, returns contact details, skills, experience, education and certifications, falls back to a section and regular-expression parser when the model is unavailable, and pre-fills rather than overwrites. Its inputs are personal details, education entries, work history with dates, certifications, uploaded documents and an uploaded résumé; its outputs are the profile, education, work experience, certification and document records, derived taxonomy skills, a derived experience total, and the completed-profile flag that opens the applicant workspace.
78
Centralized Skills Registry and Skill Evidence Module
79
Serves the applicant, who records skills, and PESO, which oversees the taxonomy. Its purpose is to hold one controlled vocabulary and to record how each claimed skill was obtained. It maintains a 789-skill taxonomy across more than 20 categories, creates unique applicant-skill links, and stores one of six provenance values: self-reported, assessment-verified, PESO-verified, résumé-extracted, training-completed, or system-seeded. A ten-question assessment engine spanning five difficulty levels records assessment evidence directly. Its inputs are skill selections from the registry and assessment answers; its outputs are applicant-skill rows carrying provenance, an updated skills string for search, and an acknowledgement from /applicant/skill-assessment/submit/.
80
Employer Profile and Accreditation Module
81
Serves the employer, which maintains the record, and PESO, which verifies it. Its purpose is to present the company to applicants and to let PESO confirm that the employer is legitimate. It records branding and logo, industry, company size, website, facility address, contact person and position, and employment types offered, and it exposes a PESO verify action. Its inputs are the company details and logo; its outputs are the employer profile fields, the verification flag, and a public company profile page that applicants can read.
82
Vacancy Management and Required-Skill Specification Module
83
Serves the employer, which owns the vacancy, and PESO, which provides oversight. Its purpose is to publish vacancies whose requirements are drawn from the same taxonomy the applicant side uses. It supports creation, editing, opening, closing, archiving and deletion; single posting and CSV bulk posting with a downloadable template; requirement rows written as normalised skills, including custom skills created on the fly; slot accounting; automatic closure when the last slot is reserved; and withdrawal of active applications when a vacancy is closed or archived. Its inputs are title, category, location, salary range, slots, minimum education, required certifications, required years, description, selected skills and an optional CSV file; its outputs are vacancy and requirement records, slot counters, notifications, and withdrawn referrals on closure.
84
Matching and Skill Gap Analysis Module
85
Serves all three roles, each with a different view. Its purpose is to turn a skills record and a vacancy into one explainable number plus a per-skill reason. It computes a composite score weighted at 0.60 for skills, 0.20 for education and 0.20 for experience, clamped to 0-100 and then adjusted for career alignment, and classifies each required skill as an exact, transferable or missing match. It provides radar and gap views for applicants, a ranked candidate pool with composite and skill-only percentages for employers, a printable evidence-backed scorecard, and threshold alerts. Its inputs are the applicant's skills, education, work experience, certifications and preferred job, together with the vacancy's requirements, education and experience thresholds; its outputs are the composite and sub-scores, a gap list with match type, scorecard rows, ranked lists and alerts.
86
AI-Assisted Recommendations and Qualification Explanation Module
87
Serves the applicant. Its purpose is to explain and rank options in language a job seeker can act on, without letting the model set the score. It ranks open vacancies against the canonical skill percentage, explains qualification fit as a verdict with a checklist, strengths and recommended actions, and ranks training programs against the applicant's identified gaps. Each function has a deterministic fallback, and the server replaces any score returned by the model with the canonical skill percentage. Its inputs are the profile, gaps, preferred job, bookmarks and program catalogue; its outputs are ranked recommendations with reasons, a fit assessment, and missing-skill lists.
88
Application, Referral, and Slot Control Module
89
Serves the applicant through self-application and PESO through referral. Its purpose is to convert interest into a single durable placement record. It provides one-click application and referral creation through the same code path, duplicate protection on the applicant-vacancy pair, slot reservation on creation and one-time release on decline, contact-attempt logging, and flagging of stale referrals. Its inputs are the vacancy identifier or an applicant-vacancy pair, with optional contact notes; its outputs are a referral record in Pending, a decremented slot counter, notifications and email, and an employer alert when the score crosses the threshold.
90
Interview Pipeline and Screening Scorecard Module
91
Serves the employer, which drives the pipeline, and PESO, which coordinates it. Its purpose is to move a shortlisted candidate through structured stages with a record of what happened at each one. It provides a hiring tracker and three named interview stages plus an interview-completed state, each appended to an auditable history with stage, mode, notes, venue or link, timestamp and actor; scheduling in video, phone or face-to-face mode; cancellation; and a 0-4 screening scorecard across seven weighted criteria. Its inputs are the stage, mode, schedule, notes, meeting link or venue and screening ratings; its outputs are the updated referral stage and history, interview records, notifications, and a weighted scorecard percentage.
92
Offer, Acceptance, and Pre-Employment Compliance Module
93
Serves the employer, which extends the offer; the applicant, who accepts and uploads documents; and PESO, which coordinates onboarding. Its purpose is to record offer terms, collect the statutory pre-employment documents, and hand the case to PESO. It captures the accepted position, salary, reporting date, work location, employment type and probationary period; prevents confirmation of an offer that was never sent; collects NBI clearance, medical certificate, birth certificate, diploma or transcript, previous employment certificate, TESDA certificate, and SSS, PhilHealth, Pag-IBIG and TIN numbers; and records PESO coordination notes and the confirmed reporting date. Its inputs are the offer terms, uploaded documents and government numbers; its outputs are the updated referral and profile status, stored files, and notifications to the applicant and to all PESO administrators.
94
Employment Monitoring, Probation, and Retention Reporting Module
95
Serves the employer, which records outcomes, and PESO, which monitors and reports them. Its purpose is to establish whether a placement actually started and held, and to produce counts that a public employment office can defend. It confirms report-for-work with the actual start date, distinguishes probationary from regular hiring by employment type, raises a no-show alert, records a mid-probation outcome with a check timestamp, and records a final outcome as regularly employed, probation extended with a new end date, or separated with a reason. It synchronises the applicant's profile status automatically, reports over 30-, 60-, 90- and 365-day age-of-referral windows, computes conversion and no-response rates, and collects bidirectional post-placement evaluations. Its inputs are the start date, probation outcomes, separation reason, ratings and selected timeframe; its outputs are referral and profile status, tracking and funnel views, and eight CSV exports.
96
Training Catalogue and Curriculum Authoring Module
97
Serves PESO, which authors and publishes programs. Its purpose is to define the training programs that missing skills map onto. It maintains a catalogue with provider, duration, required seat time, schedule, slots and addressed skills; provides a visual builder for modules, lessons, videos and quiz questions; generates a curriculum with AI assistance and imports one from an uploaded syllabus; aligns videos to topics; and can reset a program to the default curriculum. Its inputs are program metadata, the target topic and skills, an uploaded syllabus and media; its outputs are program records with a stored curriculum, a course preview, and published courses visible to applicants.
98
Course Player, Seat Time, Capstone Assessment, and Certification Module
99
Serves the applicant, who learns; PESO, which monitors; and the public, which verifies. Its purpose is to deliver the course, enforce the completion gate, and issue a credential a third party can check. It presents video, reading, activity and assessment lessons, validates lesson completion against the stored curriculum, accumulates active seat time from a player heartbeat, rolls progress up to the profile, scores the capstone on the server, and issues a certificate only when every lesson is complete, the required seat time is met and the capstone score reaches the passing threshold. The certificate carries a code in the form SKILLUP-TESDA-2026-XXXXXX, a downloadable copy and a QR link to the public verification page. Its inputs are lesson identifiers, heartbeat seconds, assessment answers and a certificate code; its outputs are enrolment progress, seat time, assessment results, a certificate, training-provenance skills and a verification result.
100
Company Reviews and Employer Reputation Module
101
Serves the applicant, who writes a review; the employer, which reads its own; PESO, which moderates; and the public, which reads the aggregate. Its purpose is to record what an employer was actually like to work for. It accepts a one-to-five star rating with a title, review text, pros, cons and employment status, supports anonymity, and recomputes an aggregate rating and review count. Its inputs are the rating and review text; its outputs are review records and an updated average displayed on the public company profile.
102
Notifications and Transactional Email Module
103
Serves all three roles. Its purpose is to keep every actor informed of a state change without requiring them to poll the system. It creates in-app notifications for applications, referrals, interview stages, offers, hiring, probation outcomes, match alerts and course completion, with a duplicate guard; surfaces applicant notifications in the interface; provides an employer match alert page; and sends seven event emails through a four-transport delivery cascade that suppresses dummy addresses. Its inputs are the state-change events raised by the other modules; its outputs are notification records with read state, delivered emails, and per-role notification pages.
104
Analytics, Exports, and Platform Configuration Module
105
Serves PESO as its primary user and the employer for its own dashboard. Its purpose is to convert the operational record into municipal labour intelligence and an auditable configuration. It provides an executive dashboard, sectoral supply and demand, in-demand skills, an employer scorecard, a skill monitoring view, training monitoring and an employment funnel; exports eight CSV reports and a styled Excel applicant workbook; and stores matching sensitivity, strict verification and AI matching settings, writing every change to a configuration audit trail. Its inputs are the recorded applicants, employers, vacancies, referrals, enrolments and reviews, a selected timeframe, and changed setting values; its outputs are dashboard and analytics views, CSV and Excel files, and audit entries.
106
Delimitations
107
The study does not claim that SkillUp guarantees employment. A high compatibility score indicates alignment between the applicant's recorded competencies and a vacancy's benchmark skill requirements; hiring decisions remain strictly with employers, and the system does not make autonomous hiring decisions.
108
Matching and recommendation are deterministic and rule-based. The composite score combines the skill, education and experience components at weights of 0.60, 0.20 and 0.20, then applies a career-alignment adjustment. It should not be read as evaluating personality traits, cultural fit, or any factor not captured in structured data. The weights are set by the researchers and are not learned from placement outcomes, and no demographic bias evaluation of the scoring function has been performed.
109
The system is not a payroll, compensation or human-resource information system. It records an accepted salary as free text on the offer and does not compute pay, deduct contributions, process loans, administer benefits, or maintain attendance, leave or personnel files. Because salary is stored as a text range, it cannot be summed reliably in analytics.
110
The system does not verify government documents with the issuing agency. NBI clearance, medical certificates, SSS, PhilHealth, Pag-IBIG and BIR numbers, birth certificates and diplomas are uploaded and recorded as present or absent; nothing is transmitted to, or validated against, the NBI, SSS, PhilHealth, Pag-IBIG, BIR, Philippine Statistics Authority or TESDA. The optional identification scan at signup extracts text from an image and compares it with the name the user typed. It performs no face matching, no document forensics and no registry lookup, and it is a pre-fill convenience rather than proof of identity.
111
Matching and ranking are not machine learning. The composite score is a fixed formula with hand-set weights and a hand-curated list of transferable skill groups. A small logistic regression learns only the transferable-skill weight from referral outcomes, and in the version evaluated it was trained on 21 labelled referrals at 42.9% accuracy, below its own activation threshold; it is forced active, so the effective transferable weight is 0.275 rather than the 0.5 code default. No model learns the education or experience weights, and no model is used in production to rank candidates. References to artificial intelligence in this study therefore mean the use of a hosted large language model for extraction, drafting and explanation, not predictive modelling of candidates.
112
Skill ratings are not calibrated measurements. Proficiency levels were deliberately removed: a skill is recorded as present or absent, and the only quality signal is a provenance value recording whether it was self-reported, assessment-verified, PESO-verified, résumé-extracted, training-completed or system-seeded. The system cannot distinguish an applicant who has used a tool once from one who has used it for years, cannot weight recency, and by default counts self-reported and résumé-extracted skills in the score because strict verification is disabled in the evaluated configuration. Self-assessment accuracy is known to vary systematically with ability and gender (Adamecz et al., 2025), which is why the study does not treat a self-rating as a measurement.
113
Artificial intelligence is used for eight bounded tasks: résumé extraction, identification-document extraction, job recommendation, qualification-fit explanation, training recommendation, curriculum generation, syllabus import, and video alignment. Each has a deterministic fallback. The model never computes the compatibility percentage, decides eligibility, issues a certificate, or overrides a PESO decision; any score it returns is replaced on the server by the canonical skill percentage. Generated curricula and parsed résumés are editable, but the system does not require a review step before publication, so expert review is a procedural control exercised by PESO rather than an enforced gate.
114
The native learning management system does not replace formal TESDA trade assessment where physical machinery, supervised practical work or a licensed assessor is legally required. It delivers modular instruction, reading guides, video lessons, checkpoint quizzes and a server-scored capstone, which is foundational preparation rather than National Certificate assessment.
115
Geographic coverage is confined to Bocaue, Bulacan and its catchment. The system contains no multi-office tenancy, no regional partitioning and no barangay-level geography; a vacancy's location is a free-text field used only as a search filter, not a structured address that could support commuting-distance matching.
116
The study involves one partner PESO office and the employers that office can reach. The employer sample is therefore an availability sample of firms already engaged with that office, not a cross-section of Philippine enterprises. Because the accreditation flag does not distinguish email verification from PESO accreditation, carries no expiry and requires no evidence document, the accredited employers in the study cannot be described as vetted to an external standard.
117
Respondents are five IT experts selected by non-probability purposive sampling. Because selection is deliberate rather than random and the sample is small, the results describe the assessment of these experts and are not generalisable to the Philippine labour force, to PESO offices in other municipalities, or to employers outside the partner set. IT experts evaluate the full ISO/IEC 25010:2023 model. Job applicants, employer representatives and PESO personnel are users of the platform but not respondents, so the study does not measure end-user satisfaction or acceptance.
118
The evaluation uses the bundled demonstration database of 77 profiles, 165 vacancies, 789 taxonomy skills, 109 training programs and 72 referrals, generated by seeding commands for demonstration. It is not production data, it does not represent real vacancy volumes in Bocaue, and its distribution was chosen to exercise every pipeline stage. Findings on match quality, ranking behaviour and analytics therefore demonstrate mechanism rather than measure labour-market fit, and company names, vacancy titles and salary ranges are illustrative.
119
Data collection is confined to the orientation and evaluation window set out in the implementation plan. That window is too short to observe a training-to-placement cycle end to end and far too short to observe a 90-day retention outcome for placements made during the study. Retention findings are limited to records already held by the partner office or entered from its retrospective records, and no claim is made about the durability of placements facilitated during evaluation.
120
The system is deployed on a single DigitalOcean droplet in Singapore with one vCPU, 512 MB of RAM and 10 GB of disk, running Ubuntu 24.04, nginx, Gunicorn with two workers and SQLite, and served over plain HTTP without a domain name. This is a demonstration and small-pilot environment, not a production environment: there is no transport-layer security, no managed database, no off-host backup, no monitoring, no load balancing and no asynchronous worker. Performance is therefore reported structurally rather than as a load-test benchmark, and behaviour beyond tens to low hundreds of concurrent users was not tested.
121
The study does not attempt a Data Privacy Act compliance audit, a procurement assessment, or an accreditation review against PESO or DOLE system standards. Identified compliance gaps, including the absence of a privacy notice, a consent record, a retention schedule and an erasure workflow, are reported as limitations rather than resolved.
122
These delimitations are consistent with the objectives. The study commits to developing and evaluating an integrated skills-matching, gap-analysis and training platform for three roles in one municipality, assessed against ISO/IEC 25010:2023. Nothing in this scope statement claims national coverage, verified government credentials, learned ranking, calibrated proficiency or production readiness, and the study should be read as a design, an implementation and an evaluation of a mechanism under controlled conditions.
123
Significance of the Study
124
The results of this study may benefit the following groups.
125
Job Applicants and Job Seekers. Applicants can discover vacancies sorted by how well they match their recorded skills, see which required skills they already meet and which they lack, and apply and track applications in one place. Where gaps exist, they are directed to relevant training, which may improve their employment readiness.
126
Employers and Companies. Employers gain structured candidate information, candidate lists with compatibility scores, and a clearer view of each candidate’s strengths and deficiencies against the skills a vacancy requires. This may make screening more efficient and improve the alignment between vacancy requirements and applicant competencies, while leaving the hiring decision to the employer.
127
PESO and Employment Service Administrators. The platform provides a centralized view of applicants, employers, vacancies, referrals, training enrollment, and post-placement retention, together with labor supply and skill demand analytics and exportable placement data. This may help administrators monitor employment demand and placement activity and coordinate referrals and training.
128
Training Providers and Training Program Managers. Because training programs are linked to specific skills, aggregated skill gap and skill demand information may help program managers, such as PESO and TESDA-linked programs, see which skills are most in demand and lacking when deciding which programs to offer.
129
Future Researchers. The study offers a documented reference model and an evaluated implementation of skills-based employment matching that integrates skill gap analysis and training recommendation, which future researchers may extend, for example with more advanced recommendation techniques.
130
Theoretical Framework
131
This study is anchored on two theories and models that explain the rationale for skills-based matching and system quality evaluation. The original theoretical foundations were retained because each remains appropriate for the employment platform; their explanations were rewritten in the context of applicants, employers, and PESO administrators.
132
Human Capital and Skills Gap Theory
133
Human Capital Theory (Becker, 1964) holds that education and acquired skills drive an individual’s productivity and economic value. This study uses the theory to justify representing an applicant’s skills as a measurable asset and comparing it with the skills a job requires. By checking the applicant’s record of each required skill, SkillUp identifies mismatches between an applicant’s competencies and a vacancy’s requirements. Because the theory treats training as an investment that raises skills and employability, it also supports recommending training for identified gaps and marking the related skill as PESO-verified after certified training.
134
Technology Acceptance Model (TAM)
135
The Technology Acceptance Model (Davis, 1989) posits that technology acceptance is driven by Perceived Ease of Use (PEOU) and Perceived Usefulness (PU). In this study, TAM is used to evaluate the acceptance of SkillUp among job applicants, employer representatives, and PESO administrators, focusing on whether they find the platform easy to use, for example creating a profile, rating skills, posting a vacancy, or reviewing candidates, and useful, for example in job matching, skill gap information, candidate evaluation, and training recommendation.
136
ISO/IEC 25010:2023 Software Quality Model
137
The ISO/IEC 25010:2023 model evaluates system quality through functional suitability, performance efficiency, compatibility, interaction capability, reliability, security, maintainability, and flexibility (and their sub-characteristics). It is used to assess the technical quality of SkillUp as a web application serving multiple user roles, and it is applied primarily by IT expert evaluators.
138
Together, these frameworks guide the development, validation, and evaluation of the platform by ensuring that it is relevant to the skills-employment problem (Human Capital and Skills Gap Theory), acceptable to its users (TAM), and consistent with recognized software quality standards (ISO/IEC 25010:2023). No additional theory was introduced.
139
Integration of the Framework
141
- The three frameworks describe one chain rather than three separate justifications. Human Capital Theory explains why the skills record matters: if skills are the productive asset that a labour market rewards, then a platform that records them, makes them comparable, and points the holder towards the training that closes a specific gap has economic value for the applicant and for the employer. The Technology Acceptance Model explains what determines whether the people who must use that record will adopt it: an applicant, an employer representative, or a PESO administrator will rely on the platform only if they find it easy to use (Perceived Ease of Use) and useful for matching, gap analysis, candidate review, and training recommendation (Perceived Usefulness). ISO/IEC 25010:2023 explains how the artefact that does this is to be judged: quality is not asserted but measured against nine characteristics with defined sub-characteristics, so a claim that the platform is usable or reliable has to be supported by ratings rather than by description.
140
+ The three frameworks describe one chain rather than three separate justifications. Human Capital Theory explains why the skills record matters: if skills are the productive asset that a labour market rewards, then a platform that records them, makes them comparable, and points the holder towards the training that closes a specific gap has economic value for the applicant and for the employer. The Technology Acceptance Model explains what determines whether the people who must use that record will adopt it: an applicant, an employer representative, or a PESO administrator will rely on the platform only if they find it easy to use (Perceived Ease of Use) and useful for matching, gap analysis, candidate review, and training recommendation (Perceived Usefulness). ISO/IEC 25010:2023 explains how the artefact that does this is to be judged: quality is not asserted but measured against eight characteristics with defined sub-characteristics, so a claim that the platform is usable or reliable has to be supported by ratings rather than by description.
141
Conceptual Framework
142
This study adopts the Input-Process-Output (IPO) model with Feedback, guided by the Iterative Waterfall Model as the System Development Life Cycle (SDLC) framework. The Iterative Waterfall Model supports systematic, structured development while allowing refinements at each phase.
143
Input
144
The input of the study includes applicant profile information (personal background, education, work experience, career preferences, résumé, and credentials); applicant skills (self-reported or PESO-verified); employer and company information; vacancy information and the required skills; training program information; user requirements gathered from applicants, employers, and PESO administrators; system requirements (functional and non-functional); the evaluation frameworks (ISO/IEC 25010:2023); and the development resources (Python, Django, database, front-end tools, hardware, and software).
145
Process
146
The process follows the Iterative Waterfall SDLC through requirements analysis, system design, development, testing, and implementation. Within the platform, the process includes account registration and verification; applicant profile and skill management; employer profile and vacancy management with required-skill mapping; skill-based matching; skill gap analysis; job discovery and application processing; employer candidate review, interview scheduling, and offer and hiring; PESO referral, pre-employment compliance, and retention monitoring; training recommendation, enrollment, and certification; notifications; analytics and reporting; and system evaluation using ISO/IEC 25010:2023.
147
Output
148
The output is the developed SkillUp platform and the information it produces: matched job opportunities and compatibility scores; skill gap reports; training recommendations; application, interview, and hiring status; candidate information for employers; dashboards and exportable reports; and the evaluation results from ISO/IEC 25010:2023 [pending data collection].
149
Feedback
150
Feedback consists of testing results and IT expert evaluation results gathered after each system iteration through testing and the ISO/IEC 25010:2023 evaluation. This feedback is analyzed to identify strengths and weaknesses and is used to refine subsequent iterations. A second feedback loop exists within the platform: after PESO certifies training completion, the newly PESO-verified skill is reflected in the applicant’s subsequent matching results.
151
Variables and Relationships
152
The independent variable is the SkillUp platform as implemented: its sixteen modules, its deterministic matching function, its training loop, and its three role-specific interfaces. The dependent variables are the software quality of the platform, measured through the nine ISO/IEC 25010:2023 characteristics, and the operational outputs the platform produces, namely match scores, gap reports, training recommendations, referral status, and retention records. Applicant, employer, and vacancy characteristics act as conditions rather than controlled variables, because the mix of skills in the registry, the quality of an employer's vacancy specification, and the size of the applicant pool all change what the platform produces, and the study does not manipulate them.
154

154
Hypothesis of the Study
155
Based on the objectives of the study, the following hypotheses are proposed:
157
- H₀: The SkillUp platform does not have a significant effect on system quality and the efficiency of skills-based job matching, skill gap identification, and training recommendation, as evaluated by IT experts.
156
+ H₀: The SkillUp platform is not effective when assessed through the ISO/IEC 25010:2023 expert evaluation; its characteristic means are not significantly greater than the scale midpoint of 2.50.
158
- H₁: The SkillUp platform has a significant effect on system quality and the efficiency of skills-based job matching, skill gap identification, and training recommendation, as evaluated by IT experts.
157
+ H₁: The SkillUp platform is effective when assessed through the ISO/IEC 25010:2023 expert evaluation; its characteristic means are significantly greater than the scale midpoint of 2.50.
158
+ Effectiveness is operationalized as the ISO/IEC 25010:2023 expert evaluation. The applicant TAM results are reported descriptively and are not part of the hypothesis test.
159
Definition of Terms
160
The terms below are defined conceptually, as they are used in the literature and in Philippine labour-market policy, and operationally, as they are implemented in SkillUp. Conceptual definitions are drawn from published sources; operational definitions describe how each term is measured or represented in the system.
161
Conceptual Definitions
162
Applicant. A person seeking employment or upskilling who registers on the platform. In Philippine public employment practice the applicant is the jobseeker whose profile is referred to an employer (Department of Labor and Employment, 2016).
163
Competency. A skill, knowledge, or attitude that enables a person to perform a task to a defined standard, and which technical-vocational training is designed to develop and certify (Technical Education and Skills Development Authority, 2023).
164
Composite match percentage. A single figure summarising how well an applicant's record corresponds to a vacancy's stated requirements. It is a form of person-job fit measure, and person-job recommendation research treats the decomposability of such a measure as the condition of its usefulness to users (Tang et al., 2025).
165
Employer. An enterprise or hiring manager that posts vacancies and selects among applicants. In the public employment system the employer is a registered partner whose vacancies are published and whose placements are reported through the employment office (Department of Labor and Employment, 2016).
166
Gap analysis. The identification of the specific requirements of a role that a candidate does not yet meet. Skills-gap analysis is the diagnostic step that precedes targeted training, and its value depends on the requirements and the candidate's skills being expressed in a comparable vocabulary (Braun et al., 2024b).
167
Public Employment Service Office (PESO). A local government unit office that facilitates employment through registration, referral, training coordination, and placement monitoring, operating within the framework set by the Department of Labor and Employment (Department of Labor and Employment, 2016).
168
Referral. The formal endorsement of an applicant to a vacancy by an employment office, which creates a record that the office is expected to monitor through placement (Department of Labor and Employment, 2016).
169
Skill provenance. The record of how a claim to a skill was obtained, whether through self-report, assessment, employer or office verification, document extraction, or completed training. Because self-assessments track ability unevenly, provenance is a condition for interpreting any skills claim (Adamecz et al., 2025).
170
Transferable skill. A skill that satisfies a requirement without matching it exactly, by belonging to the same family of technologies or practices. The concept underlies competency-based matching in which partial credit is given for related evidence (Hien et al., 2024).
171
Operational Definitions
172
Applicant. A user whose profile carries the role applicant, who completes the profile wizard, records skills with provenance, applies to vacancies, and enrols in training.
173
Competency. A row in the Centralized Skills Registry, one of 789 skills across more than 20 categories, linked to an applicant through an applicant-skill record carrying a provenance value.
174
Composite match percentage. The score returned by the matching function: 0.60 of the skill component, 0.20 of the education component, and 0.20 of the experience component, clamped to 0 to 100 and then adjusted for career alignment. Each required skill is scored 1.0 for an exact match, 0.5 for a transferable match, and 0.0 for no match.
175
Employer. A user whose profile carries the role employer, who maintains a company profile, posts vacancies with required skills drawn from the registry, and moves candidates through the hiring pipeline.
176
Gap analysis. The per-skill output of the matching function, which classifies every required skill of a chosen vacancy as an exact, transferable, or missing match and displays the result as a radar chart and a gap list.
177
Public Employment Service Office (PESO). A user whose profile carries the role admin, who verifies employers, creates referrals, authors training curricula, audits pre-employment compliance, records probation and retention outcomes, and exports reports.
178
Referral. A single record linking one applicant to one vacancy, created either by the applicant's own application or by a PESO referral, and carrying the status, interview stages, offer, pre-employment documents, probation outcome, and post-placement evaluations.
179
Skill provenance. The source field on an applicant-skill record, which takes one of six values: self-reported, assessment-verified, PESO-verified, résumé-extracted, training-completed, or system-seeded.
180
Transferable skill. A required skill satisfied through a curated group in the matching function, such as SQL, front-end framework, testing, cloud, containers, version control, agile, authentication, CSS, or programming language, and scored at the transferable weight.
181
- CHAPTER 2
181
+ CHAPTER 2 — SYSTEM DEVELOPMENT AND METHODOLOGY
182
- SYSTEM DEVELOPMENT AND METHODOLOGY
182
This chapter presents how the SkillUp platform was developed and the research methodology used to evaluate it. It first describes the research design, the respondents and setting, the research instruments, the data gathering procedure, the statistical treatment, and the ethical considerations. It then covers the development approach, requirements analysis, system design, implementation, tools, schedule, responsibilities, and testing, and it serves as the technical foundation of the capstone project.
183
Research Design
184
A quantitative, descriptive approach is used to develop and evaluate SkillUp. Numerical data are collected to quantify the system’s software quality. Descriptive research is appropriate because it goes beyond data collection to include comparison, measurement, and evaluation, allowing the researchers to objectively assess the system from structured survey responses.
185
Developmental Component
186
The developmental component produced the SkillUp platform. It followed the five phases described under System Development Methodology, and each phase produced the artefact on which the next depended. The component is developmental rather than experimental because the researchers built the artefact under study; the aim was not to compare two treatments but to produce a working system and to determine whether it met its specified functions and quality criteria.
187
Descriptive Component
188
The descriptive component documented what the system does and how the evaluators responded to it. It covers the sixteen implemented modules, the three role-specific portals, the matching and gap functions, the training loop, and the compliance and retention workflow, together with the profile of the respondents and the conditions under which the evaluation was conducted. Recording those conditions matters because the evaluation took place on a demonstration deployment with five IT experts rather than under a controlled comparison.
189
Quantitative Component
190
The quantitative component used the validated evaluation questionnaire to rate the platform against the nine ISO/IEC 25010:2023 characteristics on a four-point scale. Responses were summarised as weighted means per indicator, per characteristic, and overall, and interpreted against fixed intervals. The design is cross-sectional: each evaluator completed the instrument once, after examining all three role interfaces. It is neither longitudinal nor controlled, so the results describe the platform at the point of evaluation.
191
Why the Design Fits
193
- The design meets the three demands of the study. The developmental demand is met by building and testing the platform, the descriptive demand by documenting the implemented modules and the evaluation setting, and the quantitative demand by measuring quality with a structured instrument. The design also permits triangulation: the functional test results, the expert quality ratings, and the recorded system behaviour are different forms of evidence, and agreement among them is more informative than any single source. A high quality rating cannot compensate for a failed test case, and a passing test cannot establish that evaluators found the system usable.Research Subjects
192
+ The design meets the three demands of the study. The developmental demand is met by building and testing the platform, the descriptive demand by documenting the implemented modules and the evaluation setting, and the quantitative demand by measuring quality with a structured instrument. The design also permits triangulation: the functional test results, the expert quality ratings, and the recorded system behaviour are different forms of evidence, and agreement among them is more informative than any single source. A high quality rating cannot compensate for a failed test case, and a passing test cannot establish that evaluators found the system usable.
194
- This study uses a quantitative research design; therefore, the participants are referred to as respondents. The respondents are IT experts, who evaluate the technical quality of SkillUp. A total of 5 IT experts will participate (Table 1). Job applicants, employer representatives, and PESO administrators are the intended users of the platform, but they are not respondents of this study. Each IT expert examines the features of all three user roles and evaluates the full set of ISO/IEC 25010:2023 characteristics: functional suitability, performance efficiency, compatibility, interaction capability, reliability, security, maintainability, and flexibility.

Research Instruments
206
- The study uses one data-gathering instrument: a structured evaluation questionnaire. It is originally designed rather than adopted or adapted from an existing standardised tool: the researchers built it from the objectives of the study and aligned it with the nine characteristics of the ISO/IEC 25010:2023 software quality model. It is divided into three parts. Part I records the respondent's professional profile, namely current position, area of expertise, and years of experience in software development or software quality. Part II holds closed-ended statements on functional suitability and interaction capability, asking whether the system performs the functions each user role requires and whether it can be used effectively. Part III holds closed-ended statements on reliability and performance efficiency, asking whether the system returns consistent results and responds within an acceptable time. The IT experts additionally rate the remaining characteristics, namely compatibility, security, maintainability, and flexibility, so that all nine are covered. Every item is a closed-ended statement rated on a four-point Likert scale (4 = Excellent, 3 = Good, 2 = Fair, 1 = Poor); no open-ended or interview item is used, and the items ask respondents to judge a named feature of the system rather than to describe their own practice.
210
+ The study used two instruments. The Technology Acceptance Model (TAM) questionnaire measured applicant acceptance of SkillUp. The ISO/IEC 25010:2023 evaluation questionnaire measured the software quality of the platform as judged by IT experts. Both instruments used the same four-point agreement scale and were administered separately.

One PESO response was received, but a single response cannot support an evaluation and is excluded from the results. TAM therefore measures only the applicant entity; the ISO evaluation measures the expert view of the platform as a whole.
