Data Gathering Procedure
224
First, the researchers secure permission from the adviser and from the participating organizations to conduct the study and identify respondents using purposive sampling. Respondents are informed of the purpose of the study and their role in the evaluation, and their consent is obtained.
225
Next, the IT experts examine the developed system across all three user roles. As job applicants, they create a profile, add their skills, view job matches and skill gaps, apply for a vacancy, and view training recommendations. As employer representatives, they create a company profile and vacancy with required skills, review candidates and their gap analysis, and use the interview and hiring functions. As PESO administrators, they use verification, referral, training, tracking, and reporting functions. After examining the system, they answer the questionnaire to evaluate its software quality characteristics, either in printed form or through online forms depending on accessibility.
226
The researchers then collect and review the questionnaires for completeness and validity and encode and then organize the responses using the Statistical Package for the Social Sciences (SPSS). Descriptive statistics are used to analyze the data according to the objectives of the study. All data are used strictly for academic purposes and handled confidentially.
227
Data Analysis and Statistical Treatment
228
The collected data are analyzed using the following statistical methods, chosen according to the Statement of the Problem. Results are computed for each ISO/IEC 25010:2023 characteristic and, where appropriate, overall.
229
Frequency and Percentage Distribution: used to describe the profile of respondents.
230
Weighted Mean: used to evaluate the system in terms of usability, functional suitability, reliability, performance efficiency, and the other ISO/IEC 25010:2023 characteristics.
231
Standard Deviation: used to measure the variability of responses.
232
Cronbach’s Alpha: used to test the reliability of the questionnaire.
231
- One-Sample t-Test: used to test the null hypothesis by comparing the mean rating for each ISO/IEC 25010:2023 characteristic against the scale midpoint of 2.50.
233
+ One-Sample t-Test: used as a benchmark to compare each characteristic’s evaluator-level mean against the scale midpoint of 2.50. Because there are only five evaluators, this test is read as a descriptive benchmark, not as a population-level effect test.
232
- The study uses a four-point Likert scale. For the ISO/IEC 25010:2023 evaluation, respondents choose Excellent (4), Good (3), Fair (2), or Poor (1). The weighted mean is computed per item, and the following scale is used to interpret it.
234
+ The study uses a four-point agreement scale. For the ISO/IEC 25010:2023 evaluation, respondents choose Strongly Agree (4), Agree (3), Disagree (2), or Strongly Disagree (1). The weighted mean is computed per item, and the following scale is used to interpret it.
235
A characteristic whose mean is significantly greater than 2.50 at the 0.05 level of significance is interpreted as meeting the acceptability threshold. Where the normality assumption is not met, the Wilcoxon signed-rank test is substituted for the one-sample t-test.
236
The mean is computed with the formula μ = (Σfx) / n, where μ is the mean of each evaluation; Σfx is the sum of the products of f, the frequency of each weight, and x, the weight (4, 3, 2, or 1); and n is the total number of respondents.
237
Expert Ratings
236
- The five IT experts provided one rating per statement together with an overall judgement. The analysis computed the weighted mean and the standard deviation for each indicator, for each characteristic, and for the grand mean across characteristics. No inferential test was applied to the expert ratings, because the panel was purposive and small; the ratings are interpreted descriptively and read alongside the functional test results rather than tested for significance against each other.
238
+ The five IT experts provided one rating per statement together with an overall judgement. The analysis computed the weighted mean and the standard deviation for each indicator, for each characteristic, and for the grand mean across characteristics. A one-sample t-test was then used only as a benchmark against the 2.50 midpoint. Each evaluator’s mean for a characteristic formed one observation, giving five observations and four degrees of freedom per characteristic. Because the panel was purposive and small, the test is interpreted as a descriptive benchmark alongside the functional test results, not as a population-level estimate.
239
Functional Test Data
240
Functional testing produced pass or fail outcomes rather than survey scores. The outcomes were summarised by module and by test case, and they were used to contextualise the expert ratings: a module that failed a case was noted even where the corresponding quality rating was high, and a characteristic that drew a high rating was read against the cases that exercised it.
241
Interpretation and Limits
242
The analysis has no qualitative thematic component. Comments offered during the evaluation were used only as context for the quantitative results, not as a separately coded dataset. The study also does not model the relationship between the quality ratings and placement outcomes, because the evaluation window was too short to observe placements that any rated improvement could have influenced, and the rating therefore describes the software rather than its labour-market effect.
243
Ethical Considerations
244
Maintaining ethical integrity is essential to the conduct of this study. The researchers follow well-established ethical principles to safeguard the rights, privacy, and well-being of all respondents.
245
Ethical Approval. Before data collection, the researchers seek permission from the relevant institutional authority, the participating organizations, and the research adviser. The approval process includes a review of the research design, methodology, and ethical safeguards to minimize risks to respondents.
246
Informed Consent. All respondents receive an informed consent form that outlines the study’s purpose, procedures, potential risks, and benefits. Participation is voluntary, respondents may withdraw at any time without consequence, and they may ask questions before responding. Only those who give explicit consent are included.
247
Privacy and Confidentiality. Responses are anonymized, and names and other identifying details are not disclosed in any report. Data are stored in password-protected files accessible only to the research team. Because SkillUp accepts personal and pre-employment documents, the researchers will handle any personal data used during evaluation in accordance with applicable data privacy requirements.
248
Minimization of Harm. The study takes precautions to prevent physical, emotional, or psychological harm. Because it involves system evaluation and survey responses, risk is minimal, and respondents may skip questions or withdraw if uncomfortable.
249
Transparency and Integrity. Data are accurately recorded, analyzed, and reported without manipulation, and the findings reflect the actual responses of the respondents.
250
Responsible Data Handling and Disposal. Data are retained only as long as necessary to complete the study, then securely archived in line with institutional policy and permanently deleted; physical documents are shredded.
251
System Development Methodology
252
This study uses the Iterative Waterfall Model as the System Development Life Cycle (SDLC) in developing SkillUp. The Iterative Waterfall Model is a structured approach that follows a sequential progression of phases while allowing developers to revisit earlier stages for refinement. It addresses the limitations of the traditional waterfall model by incorporating feedback loops between phases.
253
Structured systems analysis and design follow a disciplined process in which each phase is completed before proceeding, ensuring clarity, control, and reduced development errors. Software engineering practice also recognizes that real-world development often requires revisiting earlier stages when issues are found during testing or implementation, and that combining structured phases with iterative feedback improves reliability, reduces defects, and enhances software quality.
252
- Figure 2.1 Iterative Waterfall Software Development Life Cycle
254
+ Figure 2.1. Iterative Waterfall Software Development Life Cycle
255
The model was chosen because SkillUp involves several interdependent, rule-based components (skill data, vacancy requirements, matching, gap analysis, application and hiring workflow, and training recommendation) that must each be validated before the next depends on them, while still allowing controlled iteration to improve the accuracy of match and gap results, the relevance of training recommendations, and the usability of each role’s portal.
256
Development followed five iterative phases. In the Requirements Analysis Phase, the researchers gathered and analyzed the needs of job applicants, employers, and PESO administrators, and identified functional and non-functional requirements. In the System Design Phase, the architecture, database structure, process flows, and user interface designs for the three role portals were developed. In the System Development Phase, the system was implemented in Python with the Django web framework, including the account, profile and skill, vacancy, matching and gap analysis, application and hiring, training, PESO coordination, notification, and analytics modules. In the Testing Phase, unit, integration, system, and user acceptance testing were planned to identify errors and verify functionality, reliability, and performance, with issues fed back into earlier phases. In the Implementation Phase, the system is deployed in a controlled environment where users are oriented to the platform, and their feedback informs further refinement.
257

Non-Functional Requirements
303
The non-functional requirements are: (1) Usability—each role has a dedicated, responsive portal with role-appropriate functions, semantic HTML and labelled form fields; a WCAG 2.1 audit is planned and has not yet been performed; (2) Reliability and Determinism—compatibility calculations are reproducible, and procedural fallbacks ensure uninterrupted operation when external AI APIs are unavailable; (3) Security—role-based view guards prevent privilege escalation, OTP verification guards account creation, PBKDF2 password hashing secures credentials, and uploaded files are restricted by type and size, with identification documents encrypted at rest; (4) Performance Efficiency—matching is computed at request time and related records are prefetched through the Django ORM; no load test has been run, and the employer dashboard's applicant-by-vacancy scoring is recorded as the scaling limit; and (5) Maintainability—the codebase is modular, with business logic, templates, static assets, and curriculum definitions cleanly separated.
304
Requirements Analysis
305
Existing System
306
In conventional practice, job seeking and recruitment are only partly supported by structured skills information. Job seekers typically look for vacancies through postings, walk-in or referral channels, and general job listings, and apply with a résumé that describes their skills in free text. Employers and employment offices screen these résumés and applications largely by hand, comparing them with the job description by reading and judgment. Because skills are not recorded on a common skills taxonomy, it is difficult for an applicant to know how well they fit a vacancy or what they would need to learn, and difficult for an employer to compare candidates consistently. Employment offices coordinating referrals, training, and follow-up must track these activities across separate records.
305
- Figure 2.2 Existing System for Job Applicants
307
+ Figure 2.2. Existing System for Job Applicants
306
- Figure 2.3 Existing System for Employers
308
+ Figure 2.3. Existing System for Employers
307
- Figure 2.4 Existing System for PESO admins
309
+ Figure 2.4. Existing System for PESO admins
310
Proposed System
311
The proposed system is SkillUp, a web-based employment and talent management platform that connects job applicants and employers through structured skills data and provides PESO administrators with coordination and municipal labor intelligence tools. It centralizes applicant profiles and recorded competencies, employer vacancies and benchmark skill requirements, deterministic compatibility matching, skill gap radar diagnostics, a three-stage recruitment workflow, a native interactive Learning Management System (LMS) with publicly verifiable certification, and post-placement probation and retention monitoring on one integrated platform.
312
Missing skills identified by the gap engine are cross-referenced with available training programs, generating ranked recommendations for applicants. Applicants self-enroll in courses managed through the native interactive Learning Management System (LMS). The LMS course player delivers video streaming lessons, technical standard operating procedure (SOP) reading guides, interactive module checkpoint quizzes, and heartbeat session timers that track time spent. Applicants complete an end-of-course capstone examination with a 70% passing threshold. Upon passing, the system records the completed course as training-verified evidence on the relevant skills, creates a certification flagged as TESDA, and issues a certificate bearing a verification code in the form SKILLUP-TESDA-2026-XXXXXX that anyone can check on the public verification page.
313
The platform employs a hybrid architecture: Google Gemini Generative AI assists with eight bounded tasks, namely résumé extraction, identification-document extraction, job recommendation, qualification-fit explanation, training recommendation, curriculum generation, syllabus import, and video alignment, each with a deterministic fallback, while all compatibility matching and deficiency calculations remain strictly rule-based and deterministic. Employers retain full hiring discretion across a structured three-stage interview pipeline. Role-specific portals present applicants, employers, and PESO administrators with their dedicated tools, while real-time in-app notifications keep all stakeholders informed of matches, interviews, offers, and compliance milestones.
312

Logical Specifications
318
Data Flow Diagram
319
The data flow diagram shows the inputs and outputs of each external entity and process. A data flow diagram uses four basic elements: processes, data stores, external entities, and data flows. A context diagram, also called a level 0 data flow diagram, presents the whole system as a single process with its external entities.
320
The level 0 diagram (Figure 2.8) shows three external entities interacting with SkillUp. The Job Applicant submits registration, profile, and skill data, applications, training enrollments, and pre-employment documents, and receives match scores, skill gap reports, training recommendations, application status, and notifications. The Employer submits company information, vacancies with required skills, interview schedules, offers, and hiring decisions, and receives candidate lists with match scores, gap breakdowns, and notifications. The PESO Administrator submits employer verifications, training program and certification records, referral updates, compliance audit results, and retention outcomes, and receives analytics, referral rosters, and exported reports.
321
The level 1 diagram (Figure 2.9) decomposes the system into the following processes: (1) Account Registration and Access, which verifies credentials and OTP and dispatches users by role; (2) Applicant Profile and Skill Management, which stores applicant profile and skill records and reads the Centralized Skills Registry; (3) Vacancy and Skill Requirement Management, which stores vacancies and their required skills; (4) Skill Matching and Gap Analysis, which reads applicant skills and vacancy requirements, computes compatibility, and writes the gap score log; (5) Application and Hiring Pipeline, which creates and updates referrals and interviews; (6) PESO Referral and Compliance Tracking, which handles pre-employment documents, start-date verification, and retention outcomes; (7) Training Recommendation, Enrollment, and Certification, which cross-references gaps with training programs, records enrollments, and marks certified skills as PESO-verified; (8) Notifications; and (9) Analytics and Reporting, which aggregates data into dashboards and CSV exports. The principal data stores are Users and Profiles, Centralized Skills, Applicant Skills, Vacancies and Skill Requirements, Referrals and Interviews, Training Programs and Enrollments, Gap Score Log, Notifications, and Job Bookmarks.
320
- Figure 2.8 DFD level 0
322
+ Figure 2.8. DFD level 0
321
- Figure 2.9 DFD level 1
323
+ Figure 2.9. DFD level 1
324
Physical Specifications
325
Physically, SkillUp is deployed as a single Django application on one Linux virtual private server: a DigitalOcean droplet in Singapore running Ubuntu 24.04 with one vCPU, 512 MB of RAM, and 10 GB of storage. Nginx listens on port 80 as the reverse proxy, serves static and media files directly from disk, denies the encrypted identification directories, and forwards every other request to Gunicorn over a unix socket, with two workers of two threads each supervised by systemd. The application server runs Python 3.12 with Django 6, and the data tier is a local SQLite database file reached through the Django ORM, with uploaded documents held in a local media directory rather than in an object store. Outbound integration is limited to three HTTPS services: the Gemini API for the eight AI-assisted functions, the email delivery cascade, and embedded YouTube lessons. The topology has no load balancer, cache tier, message queue, or replica, which is appropriate for a demonstration and a small pilot; PostgreSQL is documented as the migration path once concurrent writes exceed what a single SQLite writer can serialise.
326
System Design
327
Architectural Design
328
SkillUp is a role-based web application built on the Django framework and can be described using a three-tier client-server structure that separates presentation, application logic, and data.
329
Presentation Layer (Client Side): Semantic HTML5, CSS3, and vanilla JavaScript (ES6+) rendered in a standard web browser, with Tailwind CSS loaded from a content delivery network and charts drawn as inline SVG and CSS bars and progress rings. Django templates are segregated by role (applicant, employer, admin) so that each user sees a dedicated portal.
330
Application Layer (Server Side): The Django project (`skillup_project`) holds configuration, settings, routing, and WSGI/ASGI handlers. The core `tracker` application contains the models, views, URL routes, forms, and business logic, including role dispatch after login, the matching and gap analysis computation, course mapping, the referral (hiring and compliance) workflow, notifications, and analytics.
331
Data Layer: A relational database (SQLite) accessed through the Django ORM, together with a `media` store for candidate documents, résumés, certifications, pre-employment files, and logos.
332
User authentication uses the Django authentication system extended with custom profile models and OTP verification. The project also includes maintenance utilities: a seed script that populates sample applicants, employers, skills, vacancies, and courses; a script that resets transactional data for demonstrations; and a system check script that verifies models, routes, and database integrity.
333
Database Design
334
The database consists of normalized relational entities grouped into six domains: identity and profile; skills taxonomy; jobs and matching; placement and engagement; learning; and system and legacy configuration. The 22 models and their documented attributes are summarized below, and the relationships follow from the implemented Django schema.
333
- Table 10 Data Entities of SkillUp
335
+ Table 2.7. Data Entities of SkillUp
336
TABLE | Entity | Role in the System | Documented Attributes / Relationships
337
TABLE | User | Authentication record | One-to-one with Profile; username, email, password hash, date_joined, is_active.
338
TABLE | Profile | Role partition and identity | One-to-one with User. Roles: applicant, employer, admin. is_verified, verification_code, phone_number, address, average_company_rating, total_company_reviews_count.
339
TABLE | CentralizedSkill | Standardized taxonomy | Unique skill name, category (IT, BPO, Engineering, Healthcare, Trade/Vocational), competency description.
340
TABLE | ApplicantSkill | Applicant competencies | FK to Profile and CentralizedSkill. source ('self' vs 'PESO Verified'), last_updated.
341
TABLE | JobVacancy | Employer vacancy posting | FK to Profile (employer). title, category, location, salary_range, slots, remaining_slots, status (Open, Closed, Archived), min_education, required_experience_years. Automated withdrawal cascade on close.
342
TABLE | JobSkillRequirement | Vacancy skill benchmarks | FK to JobVacancy and CentralizedSkill. One row per vacancy-skill pair; the number of required skills per vacancy is not constrained by the system.
343
TABLE | TrainingProgram | LMS course catalog | ManyToMany to CentralizedSkill. title, provider (TESDA/PESO), duration, scheduled_date, status, slots, custom_curriculum (JSON: 10 modules, SOPs, quizzes, capstone exam).
344
TABLE | TrainingEnrollment | LMS progression and tracking | FK to Profile and TrainingProgram. status (Enrolled, Attended, No Show), progress_percentage, completed_lessons (JSON), assessment_score, assessment_results (JSON), time_spent_seconds, certificate_code (SKILLUP-TESDA-2026-XXXXXX).
345
TABLE | Referral | Placement state machine | FK to Profile and JobVacancy. status (22 values, including Pending, Interviewing, Accepted — Awaiting Onboarding, Confirmed — Onboarding, Hired — Probationary, Hired — Regular, Regularly Employed, Probation Extended, Withdrawn — Position Closed). interview_stage (Initial, Online/F2F, Final, Completed), interview_mode, interview_stage_history, accepted_salary, reporting_date, pre-employment audit flags, 30/60/90-day retention outcomes.
346
TABLE | Interview | Scheduled interview events | FK to Profile and JobVacancy. date, start_time, interview_type (video, phone, F2F), meeting_link, round_name, recruiter_notes.
347
TABLE | CompanyReview | Employer reputation and ratings | FK to Profile (employer and reviewer). rating (1-5 stars), review_title, review_text, pros, cons, employment_status, is_anonymous, timestamps.
348
TABLE | GapScoreLog | Historical compatibility snapshots | FK to Profile and JobVacancy. historical match percentage (0-100%), JSON breakdown of met versus missing skills per vacancy, written only by explicit flows, so most vacancies have no stored row.
349
TABLE | ApplicantDocument | Candidate document vault | FK to Profile. file, file_name, file_size, uploaded_at. Stores resumes, diplomas, and TESDA certificates.
350
TABLE | Notification | System transactional alerts | FK to User. message, notif_type (general, match_alert, offer_sent, offer_response), is_read, timestamp.
351
TABLE | Education | Applicant academic history | FK to Profile. institution, degree, field_of_study, start_year, end_year.
352
TABLE | WorkExperience | Applicant professional history | FK to Profile. company, position, start_date, end_date, is_current, description. Drives the derived experience total.
353
TABLE | Certification | Credentials and TESDA flags | FK to Profile. name, issuing_organization, is_tesda, certificate_file.
354
TABLE | JobBookmark | Saved vacancy | FK to Profile and JobVacancy. Used to rank recommendations and to filter job search.
355
TABLE | SystemSetting | Singleton global configuration | matching_sensitivity, strict_skill_verification, ai_job_matching, refresh_rate, updated_at.
356
TABLE | ConfigurationAudit | Audit trail for settings changes | FK to User. parameter_changed, old_value, new_value, status, timestamp.
357
TABLE | Skill, Milestone, StudyLog | Legacy standalone skill tracker | Retained from the original tracker; zero rows in the evaluated database and not used by the current workflow.
358
Process Flows
357
- Figure 2.10 ERD
359
+ Figure 2.10. ERD
360
The central workflow of SkillUp proceeds through the following stages, which also define the order in which the modules are used.
361
Registration, verification, and role dispatch. A user registers by selecting a role; the system creates an account and profile and a six-digit OTP; the user verifies the account and logs in, after which the user is routed to the applicant, employer, or PESO administrator dashboard.
362
Applicant profile and skills. The applicant completes the profile wizard, adds skills from the registry, and uploads a résumé and credentials. Skills are recorded as self-reported until verified.
363
Employer registration and vacancy creation. The employer registers a company, is subject to verification by a PESO administrator, and creates a vacancy with one or more required skills drawn from the Centralized Skills Registry. Publishing activates the matching engine.
364
Matching and skill gap analysis. The engine compares applicant skills with vacancy requirements, computes a composite compatibility percentage weighted at 0.60 for skills, 0.20 for education and 0.20 for experience with a career-alignment adjustment, and classifies each required skill as an exact, transferable or missing match. Missing skills are cross-referenced with training programs.
365
Job discovery and application. The applicant browses vacancies sorted by compatibility and applies; a referral record is created with pending status and the employer and PESO coordinators are notified.
366
Employer review, interview, and hiring. The employer reviews candidates with match scores and gap breakdowns, schedules an interview, and extends an offer; the applicant accepts; the employer marks the applicant as hired (probationary or regular) and the vacancy’s remaining slots decrease.
367
PESO referral and pre-employment compliance. The applicant uploads pre-employment documents; the PESO coordinator audits each and marks it complete, then verifies the actual start date.
368
Training recommendation, enrollment, and certification. Applicants who are not hired, or who wish to upskill, view courses mapped to their gaps and enroll; on passing the capstone examination the system records the trained skill as training-verified evidence and issues a certificate carrying a public verification code.
369
Post-placement monitoring. PESO records report-for-work confirmation, the mid-probation outcome and the final probation outcome, can report over 30-, 60-, 90- and 365-day age-of-referral windows, and can export compliance reports.
370
User Roles
369
- Table 11 User Roles of SkillUp
371
+ Table 2.8. User Roles of SkillUp
372
TABLE | Role | Type | Primary Responsibilities
373
TABLE | Job Applicant / Job Seeker | Primary user | Builds profile and uploads résumé and credentials; adds skills; inspects skill gap reports; browses matched jobs and applies; enrolls in training; uploads pre-employment requirements and confirms onboarding status
374
TABLE | Employer / Company | Primary user | Maintains company profile; posts vacancies with required skills; reviews candidates with match scores and gap breakdowns; schedules interviews; advances candidates through the pipeline; extends offers; submits post-placement evaluations
375
TABLE | PESO Administrator / Employment Officer | Administrative and coordination role | Verifies employers; monitors the applicant talent bench and conducts coordinator interviews; manages the training catalog and enrollments; certifies graduates; audits pre-employment compliance; conducts 30/60/90-day retention reviews; exports labor market analytics and compliance reports
376
User Interface Design
377
Each role has a dedicated portal. The screens are grouped by role below; captioned screenshots of the implemented screens follow the description.
378
Common screens. The Login and Signup screens authenticate users and register them with a selected role; the Verification screen accepts the six-digit OTP and supports resending the code; the Forgot Password and Reset Password screens support account recovery.
379
Applicant portal. The Applicant Dashboard summarizes the applicant’s activity. The Profile and Onboarding Wizard screens manage personal, education, work, career preference, skill, and document data. The Job Search screen lists vacancies sorted by compatibility. The Skill Gap screen shows the met and missing breakdown against a vacancy. The Applications screen tracks application status and hosts pre-employment document upload. The Training screen lists recommended courses and enrollment. The Notifications and Settings screens complete the portal.
380
Employer portal. The Employer Dashboard summarizes recruitment activity. Job Management supports vacancy creation, editing, skill mapping, open/close, and deletion. The Applicants screen shows the candidate pool with match scores, and the Applicant Detail screen shows a candidate’s profile, résumé, and radar gap chart. Gap Analysis provides a batch view for active openings. The Hiring Tracker is a Kanban pipeline, with screens for scheduling and cancelling interviews and sending offers. Company Profile and Notifications complete the portal.
381
PESO administrator portal. The Executive Dashboard presents analytics. Screens for the Applicant Directory (with match inspector, coordinator interview, status update, and CSV import), Employer Directory (verification), Vacancies (referral rosters), Training Monitoring (course details, roster, and certification), Skill Monitoring (supply and demand heatmap), Job Matching analytics, Employment Tracking (onboarding, compliance, and 30/60/90-day retention), Referral management (creation, status update, contact logging), and CSV export support coordination and reporting.

421
System Implementation
422
Deployment Strategy
423
SkillUp is deployed as a web-based application that users access through a web browser. Deployment involves installing the Django application, configuring the relational database, and loading initial data (skills registry, training catalog, and user accounts). The project's seed script can populate sample applicants, employers, skills, vacancies, and courses, and its reset script returns transactional data to a clean state for demonstrations. The deployment environment for the evaluation is a DigitalOcean droplet (Linux virtual private server), running Nginx as the reverse proxy and Gunicorn as the WSGI application server, with a local SQLite database.
424
User Orientation and Training
425
After deployment, users receive hands-on orientation in the features relevant to their role. Applicants are oriented in registering and verifying an account, building a profile, adding skills, viewing match scores and skill gaps, applying and tracking applications, and viewing and enrolling in training. Employers are oriented in registering a company, creating vacancies with required skills, reviewing candidates and their gap analysis, scheduling interviews, and using the hiring tracker. PESO administrators are oriented in verifying employers, monitoring applicants and vacancies, managing training and certification, tracking employment compliance and retention, and exporting reports.
426

428
The proposed project requires the following hardware specifications for development: a processor of at least 2.4 GHz dual-core or better; 8 GB RAM; 256 GB SSD storage; a display of 1366x768 resolution or higher; and a stable broadband internet connection.

Software Development Tools
444
Website Development
445
SkillUp was developed using Python and the Django web framework. The front end uses Semantic HTML5, CSS3, and vanilla JavaScript (ES6+), with Tailwind CSS loaded from a content delivery network and charts rendered as inline SVG and CSS bars and progress rings. The Django project is organized into the `skillup_project` configuration and the `tracker` application, whose templates are separated by role. Uploaded résumés, credentials, pre-employment files, and logos are stored in a media directory.
446
Database
447
A relational database (SQLite) is used and is accessed through the Django ORM, which defines the normalized entities described under Database Design.
448
Website Host
449
The application is a standard Django project with WSGI/ASGI handlers. The hosting environment used for development and evaluation is a DigitalOcean Droplet (Linux-based Virtual Private Server), configured with Nginx as the reverse proxy and Gunicorn as the WSGI application server.
450
Schedule Timeline
449
- The project followed the five phases described under System Development Methodology, and each phase produced the artifact on which the next depended. Requirements analysis and planning came first, producing the functional and non-functional requirements in Tables 7 to 9 and the process and data models in Figures 2.8 to 2.11. System design followed, fixing the architecture, the data model and the role interfaces described in this chapter. Development then implemented the sixteen modules, and testing ran alongside development rather than after it, so that defects were corrected before the next iteration began. Implementation and evaluation came last: the system was deployed to the DigitalOcean host, the five IT experts were oriented on the three role interfaces, and the evaluation questionnaire was administered over one week. Table 14 sets out the deployment and evaluation activities with the personnel assigned to each and their expected duration. The schedule was tracked against that table rather than against fixed calendar dates, because the evaluation window depended on the availability of the expert respondents. The estimated position of each phase across the two semesters is shown in Figure 2.11.
451

471
Installation and Configuration
472
The platform is installed on the host described under Physical Specifications. The application directory is created, a virtual environment is prepared, the dependencies in the requirements file are installed, migrations are applied to the database, and static files are collected. The environment variables for the secret key, the allowed hosts, the database, and the optional Gemini and email credentials are set before the first start, and the demonstration data are seeded only after the superuser account has been created.
473
User Orientation
474
Orientation is conducted separately for each role, as described under User Orientation and Training. Applicants are walked through profile completion, skill evidence, job search, gap inspection, and enrolment; employers through vacancy creation with required skills, candidate review, interview scheduling, and offers; and PESO administrators through employer verification, referral creation, training authoring, compliance auditing, and the export of reports.
475
Pilot Implementation
476
The pilot runs with the partner PESO office and a small set of employers, using seeded or imported records so that ranking, referral, and training flows can be exercised without exposing real applicant documents. Pilot findings are logged as defects or as configuration changes rather than counted as evaluation results, so that the evaluation reported in Chapter 3 remains the record of the system as it stood at assessment.
477
Monitoring and Evaluation
478
Monitoring uses the employment-tracking and analytics views: conversion and no-response rates, stale referrals, the placement funnel, and the skill supply and demand tables. Evaluation follows the design described in this chapter, with IT experts rating the platform against the nine ISO/IEC 25010:2023 characteristics after examining all three role interfaces.
479
Maintenance
480
Maintenance is scheduled rather than continuous. It covers dependency updates, database backup and a periodic restore drill, review of the skill taxonomy against the vacancies employers actually post, and review of the AI-generated curricula that PESO publishes. Every change to the scoring configuration is recorded in the audit trail so that a change in ranking can be traced to the setting that caused it.
481
Verification, Validation and Testing
482
Verification Process
483
Verification determined whether the system was built according to its specified requirements and design. It was carried out by reviewing the requirement tables against the implemented routes and models, by inspecting the role guards and the scoring function against their written specification, and by confirming that every module in the module register could be reached and exercised. Verification is a check on the build rather than on the users' judgement of it, which is why it is reported separately from validation.
484
Validation Process
483
- Validation determined whether the system met the needs of its intended users. It was carried out with the IT experts, who examined all three role interfaces and rated the platform against the nine quality characteristics, and its results are reported in Chapter 3. Validation was not limited to whether a page loads: it asked whether an applicant can see why a vacancy does or does not fit, whether an employer can compare candidates on evidence rather than on impression, and whether PESO can follow a referral from application through probation.
485
+ Validation determined whether the system met the needs of its intended users. It was carried out with the IT experts, who examined all three role interfaces and rated the platform against the eight quality characteristics, and its results are reported in Chapter 3. Validation was not limited to whether a page loads: it asked whether an applicant can see why a vacancy does or does not fit, whether an employer can compare candidates on evidence rather than on impression, and whether PESO can follow a referral from application through probation.
486
To adhere to standard software quality, the researchers use the ISO/IEC 25010:2023 Software Quality Model, established by the International Organization for Standardization (ISO) and the International Electrotechnical Commission (IEC), which offers a structured method for evaluating and quantifying software quality. The following primary quality characteristics are used:
487
Functional Suitability
488
Reliability
489
Performance Efficiency
488
- Interaction Capability (Usability)
490
+ Interaction Capability
491
Security
492
Compatibility
493
Maintainability
494
Flexibility
495
Each characteristic covers the following sub-characteristics:
496
Functional Suitability: the extent to which the system fulfills the specified functional requirements (Functional Completeness, Functional Correctness, and Functional Appropriateness).
497
Performance Efficiency: performance relative to resource utilization (Time Behavior, Resource Utilization, and Capacity).
498
Compatibility: the ability to share information and integrate with other systems (Co-existence and Interoperability).
497
- Interaction Capability (Usability): the degree to which the system can be used by specified users to meet their needs effectively and efficiently (Appropriateness Recognisability, Learnability, Operability, User Error Protection, User Engagement, Inclusivity, User Assistance, and Self-Descriptiveness).
499
+ Interaction Capability: the degree to which the system can be used by specified users to meet their needs effectively and efficiently (Appropriateness Recognisability, Learnability, Operability, User Error Protection, User Engagement, Inclusivity, User Assistance, and Self-Descriptiveness).
500
Reliability: the ability to maintain consistent performance (Availability, Fault Tolerance, and Recoverability).
501
Security: measures to protect information and data (Confidentiality, Integrity, Authenticity, and Resistance).
502
Maintainability: the capacity for modification (Modularity, Reusability, Analyzability, Modifiability, and Testability).
503
Flexibility: the degree to which the system can adapt to changes in requirements, environments and context of use (Adaptability, Scalability, Installability, and Replaceability).
504
Unit Testing
505
Unit testing is conducted at the module level to verify that each component of SkillUp performs its intended function independently. The research team uses structured test cases for each module: Account Registration, Verification, and Access; Applicant Profile and Skill Management; Job Vacancy and Skill Requirement; Skill-Based Matching and Skill Gap Analysis; Job Application and Hiring Pipeline; Training Recommendation, Enrollment, and Certification; PESO Coordination and Compliance; and Analytics and Reporting.

- Table 15 Unit Test Case Summary

TABLE | Module | Test Case | Expected Result | Status
508
TABLE | Account Access | Enter valid 6-digit OTP | User verified; automatically redirected to role-specific dashboard | Passed
509
TABLE | AI Resume Parser | Upload PDF/DOCX formatted resume | Gemini LLM extracts contact information, education, work history, and skills | Passed

TABLE | Parser Fallback | Upload resume with API key disabled | Regex heuristic fallback extracts email, phone number, and known skills | Passed

546
User Acceptance Testing (UAT)
547
User Acceptance Testing is the final phase of testing. It involves the IT experts, who act in the roles of job applicant, employer representative, and PESO administrator and interact with the system under realistic scenarios to validate that it meets the practical needs of each role. They assess the full set of ISO/IEC 25010:2023 software quality characteristics.
546
- CHAPTER 3
547
- RESULTS, DISCUSSION, AND INTERPRETATION OF DATA
548
- This chapter presents the results of the study, explains how SkillUp performed, and interprets the data gathered during testing and evaluation. The results are presented in the order of the objectives stated in Chapter 1, so that each finding can be traced to the objective it addresses. The data come from two sources: the system testing, which verified that the platform’s functions work as specified, and the respondents’ evaluation, in which IT experts rated the system’s quality after examining it.
548
Test Environment and Participants
549
Testing and evaluation were conducted on the deployment described under Physical Specifications, over a web browser, with the demonstration database in place. The participants were five IT professionals from industry and academe who examined the three role interfaces, and their profile is reported in Chapter 2. The test environment is therefore the same environment in which the system would be demonstrated, which makes the results representative of that setting but not of a production deployment under load.
550
Defect Handling and Regression
551
A case passed only when the observed behaviour matched the stated expected result; a partial result was recorded as a defect rather than as a pass. Defects were returned to the phase that caused them, so a requirement gap returned to requirements analysis, a structural fault to design, and a coding fault to implementation. The corrected build was then re-tested, and the tests that had already passed were re-run so that a fix did not break a working path.
552
+ CHAPTER 3 — RESULTS, DISCUSSION, AND INTERPRETATION OF DATA
553
+ This chapter presents the results of the study, explains how SkillUp performed, and interprets the data gathered during testing and evaluation. The results are presented in the order of the objectives stated in Chapter 1, so that each finding can be traced to the objective it addresses. The data come from two sources: the system testing, which verified that the platform’s functions work as specified, and the respondents’ evaluation, in which IT experts rated the system’s quality after examining it.
554
System Testing Results
555
Functional Testing
555
- Functional testing verified that the system does what the requirements specify. Unit testing exercised the sixteen modules individually: account access, résumé parsing and its fallback, skill inventory, the diagnostic assessment, vacancy management, matching and gap analysis, the interview pipeline, slot decrement, the course player, the capstone examination, certificate verification, and retention auditing. Integration testing then confirmed that the modules operate together: role guards hold, a parsed résumé populates the profile, a published vacancy enters the ranked search, a gap links to an aligned course, passing the capstone changes the provenance of the relevant skill, and closing a vacancy withdraws active applications. System testing exercised the complete platform against the nine quality characteristics. Every case in the three plans passed, as recorded in Tables 15 to 17.
556
+ Functional testing verified that the system does what the requirements specify. Unit testing exercised the sixteen modules individually: account access, résumé parsing and its fallback, skill inventory, the diagnostic assessment, vacancy management, matching and gap analysis, the interview pipeline, slot decrement, the course player, the capstone examination, certificate verification, and retention auditing. Integration testing then confirmed that the modules operate together: role guards hold, a parsed résumé populates the profile, a published vacancy enters the ranked search, a gap links to an aligned course, passing the capstone changes the provenance of the relevant skill, and closing a vacancy withdraws active applications. System testing exercised the complete platform against the eight quality characteristics. Every case in the three plans passed, as recorded in Tables 2.12 to 2.14.
557
Non-Functional Testing
558
Non-functional testing examined the properties that the functional cases cannot show. Determinism was tested by repeating an identical compatibility computation ten times and confirming that the score did not change. Access control was tested by attempting to reach each role's pages from another role, and every attempt was refused. Compatibility was tested by rendering the three portals in Chrome, Edge, Firefox, and Safari, where the layouts and charts were consistent. Performance was assessed structurally rather than by load test: at the evaluated volume of 62 applicants and 164 open vacancies, every portal responded within the demonstration threshold, and the applicant-by-vacancy cost of the employer dashboard was recorded as the scaling limit. Reliability under continuous load and recovery from an interrupted connection remain the least demonstrated properties, which is consistent with the Reliability rating reported in the evaluation.

Results and Discussion
672
- The evaluation results are consistent with the literature reviewed in Chapter 1 and with the design decisions described in Chapter 2. Three findings merit discussion. First, Interaction Capability obtained the highest rating at 3.76. This is the expected outcome of a design in which every result is accompanied by its explanation: the score, the three component scores, and the per-skill breakdown are presented together rather than as a single figure, which is the condition that person-job recommendation research identifies as necessary for a recommendation to be usable (Tang et al., 2025). Second, Reliability obtained the lowest rating at 3.56, although still within the Excellent band. This is consistent with the deployment described in Chapter 2: SkillUp runs on a single host with a local database and no asynchronous worker, so behaviour under continuous load and recovery from interruption are the system's least demonstrated properties. The result is therefore a finding about the deployment rather than about the matching logic. Third, the narrow spread of the nine characteristic means, from 3.56 to 3.76, indicates that no single quality attribute was allowed to compensate for another, which is what the ISO/IEC 25010:2023 model is intended to detect.
661

- A dash in Table 29 indicates that the reviewed source does not report the feature; it does not indicate that the feature is absent from the system.
679
+ A dash in Table 3.11 indicates that the reviewed source does not report the feature; it does not indicate that the feature is absent from the system.
680
PESMATCH. PESMATCH established that a municipal employment office will adopt a matching tool and that a percentage is an intelligible output for its staff (Ycoy et al., 2022). It matches on preference and qualification as declared, however, and does not represent the requirements of a vacancy as normalised skills. SkillUp differs in treating the vacancy as a set of required skills drawn from the same registry the applicant uses, which is what makes a per-skill gap possible.
681
PhilJobNet and the PESO Employment Information System. The national platform supplies the regulatory frame within which SkillUp operates: it defines how vacancies are registered, how applicants are referred, and how placements are reported (Department of Labor and Employment, 2016). Its documented limitation is that vacancy skills are not expressed in a taxonomy shared with the training system, which is precisely the gap that SkillUp closes internally (Bayudan-Dacuycuy et al., 2024). SkillUp is therefore complementary to the national system rather than a replacement for it, and does not claim to transmit records to it.
682
IT career course recommender. Hien et al. (2024) demonstrate that a rule-driven recommender can derive courses from a learner's identified gaps and career goals. The difference is the source of the requirements: SkillUp derives them from a live vacancy posted by a local employer, so the skill a person is trained for is the skill that a specific employer has asked for. That difference also carries a limitation, since a vacancy-derived gap is only as reliable as the employer's specification of the vacancy.
683
Industry upskilling tool. Braun et al. (2024a) describe a tool that identifies an individual's skill gaps and matches them to training modules within a Swedish upskilling programme. SkillUp shares the gap-to-training mechanism but wraps it in a hiring pipeline and a certification gate, so that completion changes the applicant's matching profile and produces a publicly verifiable credential.
684
ESCO-based matching. Stalidis and Kyriazidou (2024) show that a standardised occupational taxonomy can be used to compare job roles with candidate skills, and also that such standards lag what employers request. SkillUp takes the same approach with a locally curated registry, which allows it to be maintained against local demand, at the cost of the external comparability that a European standard provides.
685
The comparison isolates what no reviewed system provides. PESMATCH and PhilJobNet operate the referral relationship but do not diagnose skills. The two recommenders diagnose gaps but draw their requirements from learner goals or from an upskilling programme rather than from live vacancies in an employment service. ESCO supplies a taxonomy but no workflow. Only SkillUp joins the six functions on one record: a vacancy with normalised required skills, an applicant with provenance-tagged skills, a deterministic and decomposable gap analysis over the two, a training recommendation derived from that gap, a certification that writes the trained skill back into the applicant's profile, and a placement whose probation and retention outcome is recorded against the same referral, all under the oversight of a public employment office. That join, rather than any single feature, is the contribution of the study, and it is the integration gap identified in Chapter 1.
686
Hypothesis Testing Results
698
- This section tests the hypothesis stated in Chapter 1. The null hypothesis is that the SkillUp platform does not have a significant effect on system quality and on the efficiency of the employment process; the alternative is that it does. Because the study measured quality through expert ratings rather than through placement outcomes, the hypothesis is tested on those ratings: whether the mean rating on each ISO/IEC 25010:2023 characteristic is significantly greater than 2.50, the midpoint of the four-point scale and the boundary between Fair and Good.
687
+ The hypothesis was tested as a benchmark against the scale midpoint. The null hypothesis states that the expert ratings for the eight ISO/IEC 25010:2023 characteristics are not significantly greater than 2.50; the alternative states that they are. This is a test of whether the ratings clear the midpoint, not a test of the platform’s effect on employment outcomes.
699
- A one-sample t-test was used at the .05 level of significance. The evaluator, not the individual rating, was treated as the unit of analysis: each evaluator's mean across the ratings for a characteristic formed one observation, giving five observations per characteristic and five for the overall rating, with four degrees of freedom. Treating the five evaluators as independent units avoids the inflation that would follow from counting every rating as a separate case. Cohen's d is reported as an indication of effect size. Table 30 reports the results.
688
+ A one-sample, one-tailed t-test was used at the .05 level. Each evaluator’s mean for a characteristic formed one observation, giving five observations and four degrees of freedom per characteristic. This avoids treating every item rating as an independent case. Table 3.12 reports the results.

- Two limits qualify this result. The panel was purposive and small, so the test describes these five evaluators rather than a population of IT professionals, and the large test statistics partly reflect how closely the ratings agree. More importantly, the ratings measure judged software quality. The study did not observe placements, hiring decisions, or time-to-fill, so the clause of the alternative hypothesis on the efficiency of the employment process is not tested here; it rests on the functional evidence under System Testing Results. The result should be read as evidence that the platform was judged to meet the quality standard, not as a causal estimate of its effect on employment.

- CHAPTER 4
702
+ CHAPTER 4 — CONCLUSION AND RECOMMENDATIONS
714
- CONCLUSION AND RECOMMENDATIONS
703
This chapter synthesises the study. It restates what was undertaken, summarises the findings reported in Chapter 3, draws conclusions anchored on those findings, and sets out recommendations for the stakeholders of the platform and for future researchers.
704
Summary of the Study
705
This study designed, developed, and evaluated SkillUp, a web-based employment facilitation platform that connects job applicants, employers, and a Public Employment Service Office around a single record of skills, vacancies, and placements. It set out to determine how structured skills data, automated gap analysis, native interactive training, and municipal oversight could be combined to connect applicants with vacancies and to address workforce skill shortages in Bocaue, Bulacan.
718
- The study used a quantitative, descriptive design. Five IT experts served as respondents and evaluated the system against the nine quality characteristics of the ISO/IEC 25010:2023 product quality model, using a questionnaire that had first been content-validated by five IT professionals. Development followed the Iterative Waterfall model, and the resulting platform comprises 22 data models, 92 routes, 50 role-partitioned templates, and 16 functional modules, including a skills registry, a deterministic matching engine, a three-stage hiring pipeline, a pre-employment compliance checklist, a learning management system with a capstone assessment and a publicly verifiable certificate, and a labour-market analytics module.
706
+ The study used a quantitative, descriptive design. Five IT experts served as respondents and evaluated the system against the eight quality characteristics of the ISO/IEC 25010:2023 product quality model, using a questionnaire that had first been content-validated by five IT professionals. Development followed the Iterative Waterfall model, and the resulting platform comprises 22 data models, 92 routes, 50 role-partitioned templates, and 16 functional modules, including a skills registry, a deterministic matching engine, a three-stage hiring pipeline, a pre-employment compliance checklist, a learning management system with a capstone assessment and a publicly verifiable certificate, and a labour-market analytics module.
707
Summary of Findings
708
The system was completed and verified. Unit testing covered account access, résumé parsing and its fallback, skill inventory, the diagnostic assessment, vacancy management, matching and gap analysis, the interview pipeline, slot decrement, the course player, the capstone examination, certificate verification, and retention auditing, and all listed test cases passed. Integration testing confirmed that role guards hold, that a parsed résumé populates the profile, that a published vacancy enters the ranked search, that a gap links to an aligned course, that passing the capstone changes the provenance of the relevant skill, and that closing a vacancy withdraws active applications.
721
- The instrument itself was validated before use. Across 825 ratings from five IT professionals, the instrument obtained an overall mean of 3.70, and every component, including all nine characteristics, was rated Highly Appropriate. No item-level rating fell below 3.
709
+ The evaluation of the system produced a Grand Weighted Mean of 3.59, interpreted as Strongly Agree. All eight quality characteristics fell within the Strongly Agree range. Interaction Capability obtained the highest mean at 3.72, followed by Maintainability at 3.65. Flexibility obtained the lowest mean at 3.41; Reliability was 3.54.
722
- The evaluation of the system produced a Grand Weighted Mean of 3.66, interpreted as Excellent. All nine quality characteristics fell within the Excellent range. Interaction Capability and Maintainability obtained the highest means at 3.76 each, Security and Safety obtained 3.68 each, Functional Suitability and Compatibility obtained 3.64 each, Performance Efficiency and Flexibility obtained 3.60 each, and Reliability obtained the lowest mean at 3.56.
710
The descriptive results bear on the hypotheses stated in Chapter 1. Every characteristic mean lies in the upper quarter of the four-point scale and no item mean falls below 3.20, which is consistent with the alternative hypothesis that SkillUp produces a favourable effect on system quality, skills-based matching, gap identification and training recommendation. This study therefore reports the direction and size of that effect rather than a tested probability.
711
Conclusion
712
The study set out to determine whether an integrated platform could use structured skills data to connect applicants with vacancies, identify gaps, and direct applicants to training, and the findings support the conclusion that it can. Every specific objective stated in Chapter 1 was met: the centralised taxonomy was built with provenance-tagged evidence, employers were able to publish vacancies with required skills drawn from that taxonomy, the matching engine produced deterministic and decomposable scores, missing skills were mapped to training, completion wrote the trained skill back into the applicant's profile, and PESO was given verification, compliance, retention, and analytics functions.
726
- The evaluation supports the conclusion that SkillUp is a high-quality system by the standard adopted. A Grand Weighted Mean of 3.66, with every characteristic rated Excellent, indicates that the platform satisfies its functional and non-functional requirements without a critical deficiency. The result also supports the narrower conclusion that a deterministic, explainable scoring rule can satisfy evaluators, which is consistent with the literature's concern for explainability over expressive power in hiring systems.
713
+ The evaluation supports the conclusion that SkillUp is a high-quality system by the standard adopted. A Grand Weighted Mean of 3.59, with every characteristic rated Strongly Agree, indicates that the platform satisfies its functional and non-functional requirements without a critical deficiency. The applicant TAM results were also above the midpoint, but they are reported descriptively and are not part of the hypothesis test. The result also supports the narrower conclusion that a deterministic, explainable scoring rule can satisfy evaluators, which is consistent with the literature's concern for explainability over expressive power in hiring systems.
714
Two conclusions must be stated with their limits. First, the high rating of the matching function concerns its completeness, correctness, and usefulness as judged by IT experts; it does not establish that the weights produce good hiring outcomes, because no placement outcome was used to calibrate them. Second, Reliability obtained the lowest rating, and that result concerns the deployment environment as much as the software: a single host, a local database, and the absence of an asynchronous worker are properties of the evaluated configuration and should not be read as properties of the matching logic.
715
Finally, the study concludes that the integration itself is the contribution. The value of SkillUp to a PESO does not lie in any single module, since matching tools, job portals, and learning platforms each exist separately, but in the fact that one record carries an applicant from a skills claim through a vacancy, a gap diagnosis, a training course, a certificate, and a placement whose retention can be reported.
716
Recommendations
717
For the partner PESO. The platform should be adopted first as a monitoring and referral instrument rather than as a replacement for existing practice. Staff should be trained on the referral workflow and on the interpretation of the composite score, and the office should enter enough placement outcomes to make the retention reports meaningful.
718
For employers. Employers should specify vacancy requirements carefully, because the quality of a gap analysis cannot exceed the quality of the requirement it is computed from. Employers should also record probation and retention outcomes, since those records are what make the office's placement figures defensible.
719
For applicants. Applicants should treat the gap report as a plan rather than a verdict, and should use the training catalogue to close the specific gaps a target vacancy identifies. They should also review every field extracted from an uploaded résumé before relying on it.
720
For the institution. The college may use the platform as a demonstration case for capstone work that integrates development with a recognised quality model, and may retain the validation and evaluation instruments as templates for subsequent cohorts.
721
For future researchers. The most useful next step is a longitudinal study that follows a cohort from enrolment through training, placement, and retention, so that the matching weights can be calibrated against actual outcomes instead of set by hand. A second step is a fairness audit that measures outcomes across applicant groups, since the present study excluded protected attributes from the score but did not measure the score's effects.
722
Future Work and Direction for Future Research
723
Several extensions follow directly from the limitations reported in Chapter 2. The screening rubric should become vacancy-specific, since it is currently fixed to front-end development criteria. Employer accreditation should become a distinct state with evidence and an expiry, instead of sharing one flag with email verification. Match scores should be precomputed and cached, because the employer dashboard currently recomputes every applicant against every open vacancy on each request.
724
On the infrastructure side, the platform should move to a managed relational database, place AI-assisted work behind a task queue, and be served over TLS with an off-host backup before it holds real identification or medical documents. These are conditions of deployment rather than features of the system.
725
Two research questions remain unanswered and are worth pursuing. The first is whether gap-driven training recommendation actually raises placement rates relative to untargeted enrolment; answering it requires a comparison group and a longer observation window than this study had. The second is whether a provenance-weighted score, which distinguishes an assessed skill from a self-reported one, predicts placement better than the present binary treatment of skill presence. Both questions require data that a single municipal pilot cannot supply on its own.
726
